# AGENTS.md — ai-client

**Read this first.** This is the entry point for AI agents working in this project. The codebase is small (~18k lines across 11 task modules + harness.js + utils/) and the JSDoc in each file is excellent — this doc is the map plus the hard-won gotchas; open the referenced file when you need depth.

## 1. What this is

An agentic AI client (v2.0.0, CommonJS, Node ≥ 22.19) that processes a light-novel series **volume by volume** and produces translation-support artifacts:

- `discover` task (step 0) → **series intake**: an agent is handed `SERIES_LOCATION` and works out the rest for itself — which files are volumes (and which are art books / previews / duplicates), the reading order, the series name, the source language — then names and creates each volume folder, stages the book inside, and writes the plan of record (`translation-target.json`) plus a human-readable `translation-plan.md`. See §3.5
- `glossary` task → a canonical target-language glossary (per-volume snapshots + a final copy at the series root)
- `character-voice` task → a cumulative character voice reference (speech quirks, POV markers, narration types) and per-volume POV maps
- `style-guide` task → a cumulative style guide (house-style policies for rendering source-language constructs in the target language)
- `jump-in-wiki` task → a per-volume `wiki.md` plus a "living" `shared-wiki.md` (newest copy at the series root)
- `consistency-audit` task → a final cross-artifact audit (`consistency-report.md`, PASS/FAIL sign-off before translation)
- **translation stage** (`translate` → `translate-qa` → `polish`) → the actual translation of every volume, per chapter, by a **multi-model chain** (Hy-MT2 translates; the `translate-qa` loop then runs verify (Qwen) → retranslate (Hy-MT2) rounds until every chapter passes verification — or the round cap / stall guard stops it — and Qwen polishes last) — see §8.5
- plus deterministic translation-handoff artifacts per volume: `chapters.json` + `translation-brief.md` (and the persisted extraction JSONs / glossary coverage report)

It talks to any **OpenAI-compatible endpoint** through the Vercel AI SDK + `@openharness/core`. Tool-calling agents read the sources and write the outputs themselves through sandboxed file tools.

### Quickstart

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `npx gulp discover` | Series intake only: explore `SERIES_LOCATION`, decide the series name / source language / volume order / exclusions, lay out the volume folders, write `translation-target.json` + `translation-plan.md` |
| `npx gulp glossary` | Run the glossary task (all volumes) |
| `npx gulp character-voice` | Run the character voice reference task (all volumes) |
| `npx gulp style-guide` | Run the style guide task (all volumes) |
| `npx gulp jump-in-wiki` | Run the wiki task |
| `npx gulp consistency-audit` | Run the final cross-artifact consistency audit (writes `consistency-report.md`) |
| `npx gulp translate` | Translate all volumes, per chapter (Hy-MT2 endpoint; `TRANSLATE_*` env). Refuses to start on a FAIL `consistency-report.md` or a volume with no glossary — `--allow-fail` / `--allow-no-glossary` override |
| `npx gulp verify-translate` | Source-anchored verification of the drafts, per chapter (Qwen endpoint; `VERIFY_*` env). FAILs are fixed by `retranslate`. Default-ON — `VERIFY_TRANSLATE_ENABLED=false` makes it (and `retranslate`) a no-op |
| `npx gulp retranslate` | Retranslate the chapters that FAILED verification (Hy-MT2, findings injected as correction tasks; the bad draft is not fed back) |
| `npx gulp translate-qa` | The translation QA loop: verify batch (Qwen) → retranslate batch (Hy-MT2), repeated until every chapter passes, a round retranslates nothing (stalled), or `TRANSLATE_QA_MAX_ROUNDS` (default 3) rounds run |
| `npx gulp polish` | Final Qwen polish pass per chapter (with a deterministic regression guard that rejects a polish worse than the draft) |
| `npx gulp translation-report` | Rebuild the series-level `translation-report.md` (the deterministic roll-up of every chapter's verify / ratchet / polish verdict — what the pipeline actually published) |
| `npx gulp` (default) | All nine in order: discover → glossary → character-voice → style-guide → jump-in-wiki → consistency-audit → translate → translate-qa → polish (translate-qa loops verify → retranslate internally) |
| `npm run pipeline` | **The pipeline one step at a time** (`node index.js`): each step runs in its own process (so a fix written to disk between steps IS the code the next step runs), and after every step a deterministic **post-mortem** checks what it left behind — missing output, a surviving scaffold stub, unparseable state, the wrong document shape, quarantine evidence nobody read, a stale stray, a chapter with no draft, an audit verdict of FAIL. Reports to `.postmortem/<step>.md` + `.json`, and records what the run assessed in `.postmortem/ledger.json` (`utils/ledger.js` — the run's memory of what it already tried). `--stages=a,b`, `--post-mortem=off`, `--ledger=off`, `--fail-on=high\|medium\|never`, `--list`. Passes `--force` / `--volume NN` / `--chunked` / `--dry-run` through to every step. See §3.6 |
| `... --dry-run` | No AI calls; dump the exact prompts to `.dry-run/<task>-NN.md` (`discover` previews the deterministic layout instead) |
| `... --force` | Regenerate even if outputs already exist |
| `... --volume NN` | Process a single volume (e.g. `--volume 01`) — resolved through the manifest's installment numbers, not by parsing folder names |
| `... --chunked` | Force the chapter-by-chapter fallback for multi-chapter epub volumes (the default is whole-installment processing; the fallback also triggers automatically when the whole text exceeds `SOURCE_CHUNK_THRESHOLD_CHARS`) |
| `npx gulp translate --allow-fail` | Translate despite a FAIL (or missing) `consistency-report.md` |
| `npx gulp translate --allow-no-glossary` | Translate a volume that has no `glossary.md` (no terminology law) |
| `npm run delivery` | **Where the run stopped, and what to do about it** (`node delivery.js`): the delivery manager. Reads the plan of record, what each volume folder actually holds, the nine step assessments, the run ledger and the publish report — no model call, no code access — and writes `.postmortem/delivery-plan.md` + `.json`: which step to pick up, at which volume, whether the cumulative cascade has to rebuild the tail, whether the answer is a re-run at all or a question for the diagnostics team. `--mode=report` (default) proposes and executes nothing; `--mode=act` executes the plan one step at a time through the step runner, gated by the closed action menu, the per-step intervention budget, the anti-spin ledger and the run lock, and records what it did under the newest recorded run. `--series=<dir>`, `--json`, `--no-write`. See §3.6 |
| `npm run diagnose` | **Answer a ticket** (`node diagnose.js`): the diagnostics team. `--ticket=<id>` hands one open ticket to a **read-only** agent — it gets `readFile` / `listFiles` / `grep` and nothing else, so it can open the code, the prompts and the run transcripts (the things the delivery manager may never see) and cannot write anywhere. It answers as `{cause, options, recommend, questions, ownerNote}`; every option it offers goes through the banned-option filter on the way onto the ticket, and the turn's real tool calls are cross-checked against the files its answer cites. `--open` lists what needs an answer, `--answer=<text>` replies to one of its clarifying questions (checked on what the answer CITES), `--reask` allows a second diagnosis of the same ticket. Exit 0 answered, 1 could not answer, 2 refused the request. See §3.6 and gotcha 74 |
| `npm run fix` | **Answer a ticket with a code change** (`node fix.js`): the dev team. `--ticket=<id>` hands one answered ticket (whose chosen option says `requiresCodeChange`) to an agent that CAN edit the code — inside a boundary: it may not touch the guard tables, the tests that pin them, `hooks/`, `.env`, the machine state, or the corpus, and it may not delete anything. It proposes `{files, summary, why, couldBreak, expected, verify}`; the machine then runs the pinned checks itself (`npm test` + `npm run pipeline-loop` — the agent has no shell) and compares the files it actually changed against the files the proposal names. `--open` / `--status` / `--show=<id>` read the channel, `--verify=<id>` re-runs the checks, `--commit=<id>` lands an ACCEPTED patch and `--revert=<id>` undoes a REJECTED one. The manager may only accept or reject a proposal (`delivery.js --accept-patch` / `--reject-patch`) — this command is the only role that commits. Exit 0 done, 1 the proposal failed the contract or a check failed, 2 the request was refused. See §3.6 and gotcha 75 |
| `npm run autopilot` | **The manager driving the loop** (`node autopilot.js`): asks the manager "what next?" and then types the command for the answer it gave. Every iteration it reads the state (`readWorkingState` → `planResume`), opens the ticket the triage says is the answer (`delivery.js --open-ticket`, idempotent), offers the closed menu, makes **one tool-less model call** (`utils/manager.js`), and executes the answer by spawning the account owner's own command in its own process — `delivery.js --mode=act`, `diagnose.js --ticket=…`, `delivery.js --choose=…`, `fix.js --ticket=…`, `delivery.js --accept-patch=…` — because a code fix only takes effect at a process boundary (gotcha 66) and the loop must not be able to reach past a gate. `--mode=watch` (the code default) decides, prints the decision + the whole menu + the exact command act mode would have run, and writes NOTHING; `--mode=act` drives the run. It takes no run lock of its own but refuses to act beside one. It will not accept a patch unattended unless `safeToAcceptAutomatically` vouches for it, and it never makes a Tier C move — it escalates. `--series=<dir>`, `--max-iterations=<n>` (default 12, a wall not a budget), `--json`. No `--dry-run`: watch mode is the rehearsal. Exit 0 the end was provable, 1 stopped short, 2 the request was refused. See §3.6 and gotcha 76 |
| `npm test` | Offline tests (no AI, no network): pure functions **plus** whole-task runs against the scripted model server in `test/fake-backend.js` — the real harness, the real agent loop and the real file tools, with a fake endpoint instead of a model |
| `npm run pipeline-loop` | **The whole pipeline offline**: all ten tasks in gulp order on a two-volume fixture series, driven by the scripted model in `test/fake-workflow.js`, then a second pass that must make zero calls. Records every request to `report/wire-log.md` and runs `test/prompt-audit.js` over it. Output in `/tmp/opencode/pipeline-loop` (`PIPELINE_LOOP_DIR` to move it) |
| `npm run smoke` | Live smoke test against the `.env` endpoint (`one-shot` / `research` / `fs` arg selects one check) |
| `npm run calibrate` | Live grader calibration: grades 6 known-defect / faithful translation pairs with the production verifier and reports the score distribution (`--case=<id>` for one). Not part of `npm test` — it calls the `VERIFY_*` endpoint |
| `npm start` | Ad-hoc harness CLI: `node harness.js --system "..." --text "..." [--file f --name n]` |

## 2. File map

| Path | Role |
|---|---|
| `configs/shared.js` | Shared constants extracted from task modules (`AGENT_TOOLS_NOTE`) and score-based acceptance config (`ACCEPTANCE_WINDOW_SIZE`, `PASSING_SCORE` — the one threshold every scored gate uses — plus the exceptional-consensus knobs `ACCEPTANCE_EXCEPTIONAL_SCORE` / `ACCEPTANCE_SCORE_TOLERANCE` / `ACCEPTANCE_CONFIRMATION_CHECKS` / derived `ACCEPTANCE_CONFIRMATION_MIN_SCORE`; plus `ACCEPTANCE_CONFIRM_ON_PASSING` — the "a grade that already passes earns the window's remaining samples by re-grading, not by rewriting" rule (see §3 "QA loop"); `computeRollingAverage`, `meetsAcceptanceCriteria`, `meetsExceptionalCriteria`, `isExceptionalScore`, `isAcceptedState`). The broken `ACCEPTANCE_STRATEGY` / `ACCEPTANCE_BEST_MIN_PASSES` "best" alternative was removed (it could never accept at the default window). All nine task modules import from here. Also provides `saveRollingState` / `loadRollingState` for persisting the rolling window of scores to disk (the state file also carries the run's `sourceFingerprint`, `acceptedBy` (`"rolling-window"` / `"exceptional-consensus"` / `"passing-consensus"`), the consensus confirmations, and `stalled` — the mark a QA loop writes when it stopped because a feedback pass applied nothing rather than because it ran out of iterations; see §3 "QA loop") and `isSourceStale(state, bundle)` (fail-open when either side lacks a fingerprint). Also provides `judgeTemperature` (`JUDGE_TEMPERATURE` — every call that grades text) and `judgeThinking` (`STAGE_THINKING_LEVEL` + `AI_THINKING` — the same rule for the thinking dialect, so a grader does not spend the reply budget thinking; `utils/translate.js`'s `stageThinking()` delegates to it), `STAGE_CONCURRENCY` — the one worker-count knob for every stage — and `seriesArtifactFile(name, legacyEnvKey, seriesDir)`. Also provides the un-monitored run policies (`normalizePolicy`, `ON_VOLUME_ERROR`, `ON_MISSING_PREVIOUS`, `ON_QA_LIMIT`) and `validateRequiredEnv({ dryRun })`. Also provides `structuralError(message, cause)` + `isStructuralError(err)` — the error class that overrides the skip policies (a structural failure aborts no matter what) — and its MIRROR `tooBigForOnePassError(message, cause)` + `isTooBigForOnePassError(err)` — the ONLY failure the whole-installment → chapter-by-chapter fallback acts on (see §3 "Processing mode" and gotcha 55) — and `readBoolEnv(name, default)` — the one consistent boolean-env reader (on by default, off only for an explicit falsy value; the two historical AI_THINKING readers disagreed). |
| `utils/fs.js` | Filesystem helpers: `fileExists`, `assertWrote` (rejects missing, empty, AND stub content), `isPlaceholderContent` (detects the scaffold-stub marker), `assertWroteWithFallback` (chat-reply fallback — treats an empty/stub file as missing so it can be overwritten; returns true ONLY when a file was actually missing, and callers MUST gate their recovery turn on that value; the fallback is GATED by `looksLikeArtifact` — it writes only when ONE file is missing and the reply actually looks like the document it should hold, see gotcha 58), `looksLikeArtifact` (the plausibility gate: a headed Markdown document of at least `FALLBACK_MIN_CONTENT_CHARS` characters, or a table), `hasDocumentShape` (the shape half: a Markdown heading or a table), `FALLBACK_MIN_CONTENT_CHARS` (`AGENT_RECOVERY_MIN_CHARS`, default 1000; `0` disables the gate), `assertRealOutput` (the hard stop after a recovery turn: missing/empty/stub = error), `hasRealOutput` (the idempotency skip-check — file exists AND is not empty/stub), `isPublishableArtifact` (the STRICTER rule for the four series-root copies: real AND has the shape of the document it claims to be, with a warning instead of a silent skip — gotcha 50), `inlineReferenceMessage` (inline a cumulative reference with optional truncation), `writeProvenanceSidecar` (writes the `<copy>.provenance.json` sidecar next to a series-root artifact copy), `fingerprintFiles` (a content hash of the named files — `path:length:sha256` joined, an unreadable file fingerprints as `missing` so a read error cannot masquerade as "nothing changed"; the QA loop takes it before and after a feedback pass and stops when the two match, because a pass that rewrote nothing is not an iteration — see §3 "QA loop" and gotcha 65), **`stageSourceFile` (put a source where a volume folder expects it WITHOUT a second copy — a relative shortcut to the original, falling back to a copy where this filesystem cannot link; the destination is inspected with `lstat` first so a broken shortcut is refused instead of written through, and the no-clobber content comparison still runs — see gotcha 77)**, **`shortcutTarget` (where a shortcut points and whether it resolves — the two halves a "source file not found" error needs once a staged book is a shortcut)**, `wipeAttemptOutputs` (removes ONLY the named files — plus an optional glob for a mode's per-chapter strays — before the whole→chunked fallback re-runs a volume; it never walks the folder and never touches the source bundle). |
| `utils/tokens.js` | **The one place the pipeline's token estimate lives.** `estimateTokens` / `estimateMix` (calibrated coefficients: `DEFAULT_CJK_WEIGHT` 0.69 per CJK char, `DEFAULT_OTHER_WEIGHT` 0.25 per other char, `DEFAULT_TEMPLATE_OVERHEAD` 52 per REQUEST — billed once, in `tokenBudgetFor`, never per block), `scriptMixOf` (a text's CJK-vs-everything else character counts: the half that belongs to the TEXT, measured rather than implied by a language name), `tokenEstimateMargin` (`TOKEN_ESTIMATE_MARGIN`, default 1.15 — the explicit guarantee that the estimate is an over-estimate, replacing the accidental 1.45× the old coefficients carried; sized from the measurement that a calibrated base estimate lands up to ~5% UNDER one volume's own count), `deriveCoefficients` (pure: solve the coefficients from ONE measured request — a CJK-heavy sample solves the CJK weight and keeps the fitted Latin one, a Latin-only sample scales both), the per-endpoint calibration cache (`.token-calibration.json`, keyed on `baseUrl|model`, age-guarded by `TOKEN_CALIBRATION_MAX_AGE_HOURS` because a shared-port setup gives every container the same alias — see gotcha 22), `ensureTokenCalibration` (stage-start: reuse the cache, else probe once against the volume's own text, else fall back to the built-in coefficients — FAIL-SOFT, token accounting must never be the reason a run dies), `tokenBudgetFor` (the pure budget arithmetic), `globalEndpoint` / `budgetFor` (what window and reply reserve a stage budgets against), `chunkSafetyFraction` (`SOURCE_CHUNK_SAFETY_FRACTION` — now ONE knob for both size decisions, the whole-installment one and the chapter-part one; `utils/source.js` re-exports it), `artifactGrowthFactor` (`ARTIFACT_GROWTH_FACTOR` — how much a cumulative reference is expected to grow per volume) and `answerRoom` (the ANSWER side of the size question: the expected output × the estimate margin vs the output cap the server actually enforces — see §3 "Processing mode"), `describeCoefficients`. **A token count belongs to a (text, model) pair**, which is why the bundle persists the script MIX and not a token count (see §3, gotcha 54). |
| `utils/agents.js` | The shared malformed-tool-call guard: `emittedToolCallAsText` (a turn made no real tool calls but its text contains `tool_call` / `<function=` — the intermittent local-endpoint failure) and `assertRealToolCalls(result, who, volumeLabel?)` (throws a diagnostic error for it instead of letting the stale-file write check mask the no-op turn). Used by every file-writing task (glossary, character-voice, style-guide, jump-in-wiki, consistency-audit, intake) — previously each carried its own copy. |
| `utils/prompt.js` | Prompt/verdict helpers: `transformUserPrompt`, `isPassingVerdict` (legacy binary verdict — kept for compatibility, no longer used in the acceptance path), `parseAcceptanceReply` (parses the acceptance one-shot reply — the JSON `{"score", "band", "note"}` contract first, then the legacy `parseAcceptanceScore` integer forms as a fallback; `null` = unparseable = failed check), `parseAcceptanceScore` (legacy 0–100 integer parser — the fallback half of `parseAcceptanceReply`), **the three agent step caps** — `validatorMaxStepsFor(sourceBytes)` (`max(40, 2·ceil(bytes/32KB) + 24)`), `authorMaxStepsFor(artifactSizeBytes, sourceBytes)` (a cumulative-artifact author/feedback turn reads the artifact it amends AND the text it amends from; a flat 40 is where 17 of the live run's 25 step-cap warnings came from, and the flat 30 the character-voice and style-guide stages carried is where their zero-write 46-tool-call feedback turn came from), `findingsMergeMaxStepsFor(segmentCount, artifactSizeBytes)` (the chunked findings-merge agent reads one partial per chapter plus the artifact it audits; a flat 20 lost a whole validation round on a 10-chapter volume) — all three follow what the agent must READ, because a cap that is too low throws away work already paid for (gotcha 64); `writePromptDump`, and **`selectSectionsByRelevance`** — the shared truncator for cumulative, section-organised references. It keeps the sections that MATTER to the text being processed (a heading, or a quoted source-language span, that occurs in it) and drops the rest, instead of "the last N sections" / "the first N characters", which for a cumulative document means the volume-1 main cast and the honorific rules are the first things thrown away. Used by `truncateGlossary` (via its own table-aware variant), `truncateVoiceRef`, `truncateStyleGuide`, and `loadVolumeReferences` (with `maxChars`). Without a source text it keeps the legacy window and its note SAYS so. |
| `utils/manifest.js` | JSON/manifest helpers: `extractJsonObject`, `installmentNumberFromDir` (legacy), `normalizeInstallmentNumber` ("1"/1/"007" → "01"), `sanitizeFolderName` (an agent-chosen folder name is validated, never rewritten: no separators, no `..`, no absolute paths, no Windows-illegal characters, source-language text kept), `filterVolumesByInstallment` (the `--volume NN` lookup — by the manifest's installment number, or an exact folder name). |
| `utils/source.js` | Source-bundle helpers: `resolveSourceBundle` (normalizes a volume's source into a `SourceBundle`; plain-text passes through as-is, `.epub` is extracted once and cached; every bundle carries a `sourceFingerprint` — `sha256OfFile(originalPath)` for plain text, the cache `sha256` for epub — used by the source-staleness detection, see §3, and a `scriptMix` for the whole installment plus one per segment — the character counts the token estimate is computed from, see `utils/tokens.js`), **`planProcessingMode` (the whole-installment vs chapter-by-chapter decision, in tokens: `source + injected references + instructions <= safetyFraction × roleWindow − reply reserve`, returning the mode plus the numbers and the basis it used) and `decideProcessingMode` (the same decision end to end for one volume of one stage: calibrate the estimate against the model this stage is about to run on, measure the reference material it injects, decide, LOG the numbers, and separately report the ANSWER it expects to have to write — the previous volume's artifact × `artifactGrowthFactor` against the output cap, via `tokens.answerRoom`; reported, never used to flip the mode, because a cumulative reference is still written whole at the final chapter)** — `shouldProcessChunked` is now the documented FALLBACK that rule uses when the token rule is unavailable (a bundle cached before schema 6, or a stage with no known window), `splitPlainTextSegments` (paragraph-aware, lossless split of an oversized plain-text source) + `materializeTextParts` (writes/caches the part files by source fingerprint), **`openEpub` (the single shared epub container reader: catalog card — title/creator/language/publisher/identifier plus the Calibre or EPUB3 series marker — the manifest, the spine, the readable sections and their nav titles, `navEntries` (the same nav links IN DOCUMENT ORDER, each with `inToc` and its declared `epub:type` — the book's own declaration of where its sections begin), and `payload` — the archive's uncompressed text bytes vs its other bytes, read off the zip central directory without decompressing an image; parses the OPF as XML)**, `readEpubSection` (bounded plain-text slice of one section), `htmlToPlainText`, `scriptCounts` (kana/hangul/Han/latin counts — evidence for the intake agent, not a decision), `extractEpubToBundle` (built on `openEpub`; XHTML → Markdown, then **sections are grouped into chapters by the book's own contents list** — `groupSpineIntoChapters` + `classifySectionGroup` + `titleOfUnnamedSection` + `stripRepeatedHeading` — see §3 "Source bundle" and gotcha 52; per-chapter files, interludes, epilogue, images), `assignSegmentIds`, `classifyTitle`, `xhtmlToMarkdown`, `sourceMaterialLine`, `sourceSegmentListLine`, `chapterSegmentNote`, `chapterContextBlock`, `isEpubPath`, `normalizeZipPath`, `sha256OfFile`, `plainTextTitle` (a plain-text source's own declared first-line title, marked `syntheticTitle: true` when there is none). **Extraction honesty:** `extractEpubToBundle` logs the spine accounting (how many items it was offered, how many it could read, and WHICH it skipped), logs the packaging it skipped and WHY (each entry also persisted in `bundle.packaging`), warns when the contents list omitted a section that holds real text (it is kept as a chapter, titled from what the page prints, or `Untitled section N` + `syntheticTitle: true` when it prints none), warns when the packaging it skipped is more than 10% of the chapters' text (the tell that a real chapter was cut), flags every segment with `bodyChars` and `empty` (under `SOURCE_EMPTY_SEGMENT_CHARS`, default 200 — a blank page, an image-only page, a section whose text lives in a structure the converter does not map), and warns when the per-chapter files and the whole-installment file disagree in length. A chapter that is empty IN THE SOURCE is a hole in the book, and it is reported at extraction instead of surfacing later as a mysterious translation failure. `BUNDLE_SCHEMA_VERSION` (6) is part of the extraction cache key — renaming the id scheme or changing the shape bumps it and forces a clean re-extraction (5 = chapters come from the contents list, not one per spine page; 6 = every bundle records its SCRIPT MIX, whole and per chapter, so a token estimate can be recomputed for a different model without re-reading the book). All nine task modules resolve their source through `resolveSourceBundle` at the choke point; the intake agent's epub tools go through `openEpub`. |
| `utils/handoff.js` | Deterministic per-volume translation handoff (no AI): `buildChaptersJson` (chapter list from the bundle segments, carrying `bodyChars`, `empty` and `syntheticTitle` through so a section that converted to nothing — or a section the book never named — is visible in every downstream list), `renderNewEntry`, `buildTranslationBriefMarkdown` (pure — the one-page brief: new terms/voices/style rules from the persisted extraction JSONs, chapter table (a section that is empty in the source and a section the book never titled are both marked in it), pointers to every per-volume + series-level reference artifact), `writeVolumeHandoff` (best-effort writer of `chapters.json` + `translation-brief.md`; called from jump-in-wiki.js on both the processed and skipped paths — a failure warns, never fails a volume). |
| `utils/translation-report.js` | The series-level publish gate and its report (deterministic, no AI). `checkTranslationPreconditions` is the `translate` entry gate (a FAIL `consistency-report.md`, a stale/missing audit, or a volume with no `glossary.md` stops the stage before a token is spent — `--allow-fail` / `--allow-no-glossary` override). `writeTranslationReport` reads every volume's `translation-state.json` + `translation-verification.json` + `polish-verification.json` and writes `translation-report.md` (+ `.json` + provenance sidecar) at the series root: per-chapter outcome (PUBLISHED verified / UNVERIFIED / MISSING / EMPTY IN SOURCE), verify score, retranslate attempts, ratchet state, polish verdict, and the volume-level rendering-variant counts. Called at the end of `translate`, `translate-qa` and `polish`, and available as its own gulp task. **It is also the only reader of its own output**: `readTranslationReport(seriesDir)` reads the machine-readable sidecar back (null when absent or unparseable — a roll-up that cannot be read is absent, not empty) and `summarizeReportRows(rows)` is the one roll-up of the outcome buckets, the median score, the two deterministic drift counts and the published character count. `utils/resume.js` and `utils/delivery-verify.js` both read through these, so the publish report cannot be counted two different ways by two consumers (see gotcha 73). |
| `utils/artifacts.js` | **What each step is supposed to leave behind**, written as data: per step, the per-volume files it must produce (`volume`), the series-root files it must publish (`series`), and the file patterns whose PRESENCE is a finding (`quarantines` — a `.rejected` file means a gate fired and nobody read it). Every expectation carries a `level` (`required` only where the pipeline writes the file on every path it can reach — over-declaring is worse than under-declaring, see gotcha 65), a `shape` (`table` / `document` / `json` / `any`), a one-line `why`, and an optional `when` predicate so a stage the operator turned off is not reported as missing. Also `KNOWN_VOLUME_FILE_PATTERNS` — the legitimate files no single step declares (source part files, `volume-consistency.*`, both spellings of quarantine evidence), which the unexpected-file check needs or every staged book becomes a finding. `test/test-postmortem.js` pins that the three step lists (`utils/hooks.js` TASKS, `gulpfile.js` PIPELINE_STEPS, these specs) cannot drift. The assessment lives in `utils/postmortem.js`. |
| `utils/postmortem.js` | **The deterministic "did this step finish?" check** — no model call, no network, no judgement about quality. `runPostMortem({ step, seriesDir, manifest, volumeArg })` asks the question `utils/artifacts.js` declares against the disk and returns a `PostMortemReport` of findings (`HIGH` = the step did not finish what it claims to have; `MEDIUM` = a gap worth reading; `LOW` = worth knowing). Nine checks: expected file missing; a file that is empty or still a scaffold stub; a `.json` that does not parse; a document without the shape its prompt specifies (`hasTableShape` is separate from `hasDocumentShape` because the glossary's whole contract IS a table); quarantine evidence a gate left; a volume-folder file the pipeline's own vocabulary does not recognise (the stale-stray class, gotcha 3); a rolling window that never met the acceptance criterion but published anyway (`ON_QA_LIMIT=accept`); a chapter `chapters.json` lists with no draft and no state entry (with `empty: true` chapters excluded — a hole in the BOOK is not a hole in the run); and the consistency audit's own `**FAIL**` verdict, which does not fail its task and therefore reaches nobody unless something carries it. `--volume` runs skip the series-root expectations (the tasks deliberately do not publish a stale series copy for one volume). **An assessment that could not run returns `error` + `ok: false`** — never a clean report. `writePostMortemReport` writes `<step>.md` + `<step>.json` to `postMortemDir()`. It deliberately does NOT judge whether an artifact is GOOD — that is the scored gates' job, and a second weaker grader next to the real one is a liability. See §3.6. |
| `utils/ledger.js` | **What a run has already tried, and whether it helped** — the run's memory, written as data (no model call). Nothing else in the pipeline records a DECISION: `translation-state.json`, the `*-rolling-state.json` files and the verification sidecars all record ARTIFACTS, and none of them can answer "have I already tried this?". `appendLedgerEntry` appends one entry per assessed step (`kind: "assessment"`, with the post-mortem's counts and distinct finding kinds) and, later, one per decision (`kind: "intervention"`, with the finding kind, the named action, the outcome judged by comparing the deliverable before and after, and the `signals` account — the compact before/after numbers behind that outcome, so a verdict is never the only thing recorded). `interventionAllowed(key)` is the anti-spin gate: **the same action against the same finding, ending `unchanged` or `worse` `LEDGER_SPIN_ATTEMPTS` (2) times in one run, is refused**, and the refusal names the next step (open a ticket) — the run-level form of `TRANSLATE_QA_RETRY_BUDGET`. Counted per run, so a real fix does not freeze an action out forever; only REPETITION is blocked (a different action, or a different volume, is a new attempt; an `improved` attempt is not evidence against anything). `recurringFindings(entries, run)` reports, for free, the finding classes that survived an earlier run — the signal that a problem is structural rather than transient. **A ledger that cannot be read is treated as spinning, not as empty** (refusing is recoverable; spinning is not), and `readLedger` never reports a corrupt ledger as empty. A `null` volume stays `null` in the file — coercing it with `String()` would store the string `"null"`, and `matchesKey` compares a step-level entry against a step-level key, so a whole-step action would never match itself and the gate would silently stop counting the case it exists for (`test/test-ledger.js` pins both halves). `renderLedgerMarkdown` is the human-facing summary. Written to `postMortemDir()/ledger.json` (gitignored machine state). See §3.6 and gotcha 69. |
| `utils/tickets.js` | **The only channel between the delivery manager and the teams that can see the code.** A ticket is findings-shaped and never outcome-shaped: `createTicket` takes the step, the finding `kind`, the evidence it actually looked at, what was already tried (read out of the ledger by `triedFromLedger`, not from the manager's memory), what was ruled out, and a **question** — and `validateTicketShape` refuses anything else, naming what to write instead: a command (`Disable the guard.`), a demanded result (`Make volume 15 pass`, `so that it passes`, `get rid of the finding`), a request for a banned thing, or a summary with no question in it. `attachOptions` is where the **banned-option filter** runs — on the provider's side of the conversation, because a manager given a menu picks the cheapest item on it, so a rule about the manager's judgment is not a rule. `BANNED_OPTIONS` covers turning off a carry-forward guard, `--allow-fail` / `--allow-no-glossary`, lowering `PASSING_SCORE` or any acceptance threshold, deleting quarantine evidence or reports, editing `hooks/`, declaring an artifact expectation away, flipping an `ON_*` run policy to `skip`, and disabling the ledger — each with the reason and the escalation (**the account owner**). Refused options are **kept on the ticket**, not dropped: a dropped option looks like it was never thought of. `recordChoice` requires a reason; `closeTicket` accepts only `improved` / `unchanged` / `worse` — measured on the deliverable, never `finding-gone`. The other half of the conversation is here too: `recordDiagnosis` is the **only** door from a model reply to a ticket's options (it calls `attachOptions` internally, so the filter cannot be bypassed), `recordAnswer` records the manager's reply to one of the diagnostics team's clarifying questions and refuses to re-answer one that is already closed, `unansweredQuestions` lists what the team is still waiting on, and `customerMayRead` is the table that makes a manager's answer fail when it cites `.logs/**`, a `.js`, a prompt or `hooks/` (the boundary is checked on the ANSWER, not the question). `filterOptions` stamps `outcomeOnlyVerification` on an allowed option whose only stated check is "the finding disappears", so the flag reaches the stored option and `tickets.md` rather than living in the in-memory reply. Written to `postMortemDir()/tickets.json` + `tickets.md`. See §3.6, gotcha 70 and gotcha 74. |
| `utils/diagnostics.js` | **The read-only support team** — the role that CAN see the code, answering a ticket the delivery manager wrote (one model call per ticket). `readOnlyFsTools({cwd, allowedDirs})` hands the agent **only** `readFile` / `listFiles` / `grep` out of `harness.createGatedFsTools` and returns an `approve` gate that denies every mutating call AND records each refusal — the gotcha-8 two-layer pattern, so a write attempt is reported whichever layer stopped it (`collectWriteAttempts`). `DIAGNOSIS_CONTRACT` is the reply shape it must answer in (`{cause, options:[{label, touches, cost, risk, verify, requiresCodeChange}], recommend, questions, read, ownerNote}`) and `validateDiagnosisShape` is fail-closed on it like `parseAcceptanceReply`: no cause / no options / an option missing label-touches-cost-risk-verify / a bad `cost` word / a question only a code reader can answer are **refused**; a thin cause, no cited reading, a recommendation naming no offered option, an outcome-only `verify`, a cited file the turn never opened, or state that moved during the turn are **warnings**. `crossCheckReads` compares the reply's claimed `read` list against the turn's real tool calls — a `grep`/`listFiles` over a FOLDER covers the files inside it, a call that ERRORED covers nothing, and a cited-but-unopened file is reported as `citedWithoutReading`. `diagnoseTicket` fingerprints the ticket's evidence before and after the turn (`stateMovedDuringDiagnosis` — read-only checked against the disk, not asserted), scales the step cap by what the ticket points at (`diagnosticsMaxStepsFor`: 3 steps per 32 KB page + 24, floor 30, `DIAGNOSIS_STEP_CAP_CEILING` 120 — the auditor stays cheaper than the stage it audits), and reaches the ticket only through `recordDiagnosis`, which runs the banned-option filter internally so no path attaches a diagnosis that skips it. `ownerNote` is where "I actually believe the guard is wrong" belongs: prose for the **account owner**, never an option. See §3.6 and gotcha 74. |
| `utils/resume.js` | **Where the run stopped, written as data** — the delivery manager's triage (no model call, no code access). `DELIVERY_ACTIONS` is the **closed menu**: every move the manager may propose, as an entry with a `tier` (A = act alone, B = capped, C = never), the primitive it maps to, and `countsAsIntervention` on each one — so "picking up unfinished work is free, destroying finished work is an intervention" is a field the code can act on rather than a sentence in a prompt. Tier C entries carry a `why`, so a refusal can name what it refused, and `actionIsAvailable(name)` refuses anything not on the menu. `readPlanOfRecord` reads `translation-target.json` + `validateManifest` **directly** (never `getTranslationTarget()`, so a triage cannot spend a token or start the intake agent). `inventoryVolume` compares each volume folder against `STEP_ARTIFACT_SPECS` with `{installment}` resolved, and attributes each `.rejected` file to the step whose **artifact it names** (the three cumulative steps share one quarantine pattern, so matching the pattern alone calls a `character-voice.md.rejected` glossary's evidence). `readWorkingState` runs one `runPostMortem` per `PIPELINE_STEPS` entry and splits the HIGH findings into **damage** (work is missing — this decides where to pick up) and **evidence** (a gate refused something — read it, it is not a reason to rebuild); anything HIGH that is not explicitly evidence counts as damage, so a new finding kind cannot become invisible. `planResume` then decides: `resume` / `nothing-to-do` / `evidence` / `blocked`; the wipe list is the resume step's declared outputs only (`.rejected` evidence is never wiped, Tier C); a FAIL audit verdict and a finding the ledger says already survived a run both become **`open-ticket`, not a re-run**; a volume missing a step's output **while holding that step's own quarantine** is the volume-15 shape and becomes a ticket too, because re-running a deterministic gate reproduces the identical quarantine (gotcha 68); later steps report `after` / `blocked` with their own earliest damaged volume; a cumulative cascade is never proposed with `--volume` (gotcha 66). `formatRanges` + `progressByStep` render "glossary is whole through 14, missing 15–17" instead of seventeen folders of file names. `maxInterventionsPerStep` (`DELIVERY_MAX_INTERVENTIONS`, default 5) and `interventionsUsed` read the **per-step** budget out of the ledger's `intervention` entries for the newest recorded run — counting only the menu entries marked `countsAsIntervention` — and `planResume` turns a step that has spent it into `open-ticket`. Every plan line carries the `finding` kind the action is a response to, because the anti-spin gate keys on it; a step that is complete and is only in the plan because the resume step's fix invalidates it **inherits the finding the whole plan is a response to**, since otherwise the memory has nothing to key on and "have I already run this step in this sequence, and did it help?" becomes unanswerable. `readWorkingState` also keeps the HIGH findings themselves on the step state (`damageFindings` / `evidenceFindings`, each with `kind` / `file` / `volume` / `message`) — a ticket must cite what the manager looked at, and this assessment is the only thing it is allowed to have looked at. `readDeliverable` reads the publish report through `utils/translation-report.js`'s own reader and roll-up, so the triage and the before/after comparison cannot count one report two ways. `progressForStep(volumes, step)` is still exported for reporting a step's progress; act mode's verdict comes from `utils/delivery-verify.js`. **The triage reads its own correspondence**: `readWorkingState` attaches `tickets` / `ticketsError` / `patches` / `patchesError` so `planResume` stays a pure function of a state snapshot (a hand-built test state must not silently read the live series' history — gotcha 71), and `planResume` computes the repair shape FIRST and then runs the three "re-running reproduces the same result" checks through one `escalate(kind, subject, reasons)` helper that either overwrites the plan with `open-ticket` or is **superseded**: an existing non-closed ticket for that exact `(step, volume, finding)` is named on the plan line (`plan.existingTicket`) instead of a duplicate being proposed; an `accepted` or `committed` patch answering it turns the escalation back into `run` / `wipe-and-cascade` with a reason naming both (an `accepted` one also says the commit is still owed); a ticket closed `unchanged` / `worse` keeps the escalation and says time passing does not make a re-run legitimate; and a `proposed` patch answers nothing, because unjudged code in the tree is what gates act mode. The supersede is deliberately **not** applied to `intervention-budget` — a code change cannot un-spend attempts this run already made, and applying the patch is itself the counted wipe-and-cascade — so that escalation stands and names the account owner as the one who raises `DELIVERY_MAX_INTERVENTIONS`. `ESCALATION_HEADLINES` keys the headline on `plan.action`, so a superseded gate-removed escalation reads as a run. See §3.6 and gotcha 71. |
| `utils/delivery-verify.js` | **Did the intervention help the book?** — the acceptance test for an intervention (deterministic, no model call). `measureDeliverable({seriesDir, volumes})` takes one measurement of the deliverable: the publish report's roll-up (read through `utils/translation-report.js`), what each cumulative artifact actually holds (glossary term rows, `### Character` sections, `## ` style categories, the `chapters.json` handoff count, and the newest snapshot every later volume reads), and how many volumes hold each step's declared output. `compareDeliverable(before, after)` answers `improved` / `unchanged` / `worse` from 18 named signals, each declared as data with a direction, a reason and a `veto` flag. Three rules decide it, and they are the module: **a veto-eligible regression outranks any number of improvements** (the planted fix that finishes the step while shrinking the glossary is `worse`); **a gate's verdict is never a signal** — PASS/FAIL reports, validation reports and quarantine counts are excluded, because switching a check off moves them for free, which is the difference between "the complaint stopped" and "the book got better"; and **an incomplete measurement is reported, not guessed** — a file that could not be read makes that step's content signals not-comparable rather than zero, and the median verification score is compared only across the same chapter count. `accountOf` and `describeComparison` print what moved (a verdict with no account next to it is not arguable), `summarizeSnapshot` is the compact account the ledger stores, and `closureFromComparison` produces the payload `utils/tickets.js` accepts, so act mode's ledger entry and a ticket's closure come from one comparison. `styleRules` is deliberately `veto: false` — the style guide's own gate reports a dropped rule count rather than failing on it (gotcha 65). See §3.6 and gotcha 73. |
| `delivery.js` | **The delivery manager's CLI** over `utils/resume.js` — thin on purpose: it reads a state, prints the plan, writes it, and in `act` mode executes it. `--mode=report` (default, from `DELIVERY_MODE`) or `--mode=act`; `--series=<dir>`; `--json`; `--no-write`; an unknown flag is refused with exit 2, because a mistyped flag on a tool that reads a live series should fail. Every action the plan names is re-checked against `DELIVERY_ACTIONS` (exit 2 if it is not available), so a future edit to `planResume` cannot invent a move. **Act mode** gates each step (menu → per-step budget → anti-spin), wipes the step's declared outputs, spawns the real step runner (`node index.js --stages=<step>` with `INDEX_RUN_ID` and `SERIES_LOCATION` set explicitly), re-reads the state, judges the outcome by comparing the deliverable before and after (`utils/delivery-verify.js` — `improved` / `unchanged` / `worse`, with the account of what moved), records the attempt in the ledger under the **newest recorded run** (so the anti-spin gate can see the whole story) together with the before/after signal account, opens a ticket when a gate refuses, and stops the sequence at the first refusal or failed step. `closeTicketOnDeliverable` closes a ticket by re-measuring now and comparing against the snapshot taken before the fix, through the same `closureFromComparison` act mode writes to the ledger — so the manager cannot record `worse` in one place and `improved` in the other. It refuses to run while a pipeline run holds the run lock, refuses `--no-write` together with `--mode=act` (exit 2 — a rehearsal that records is a contradiction), and executes nothing when the plan's own answer for the resume point is a ticket. **The three verbs on the escalation ladder** are the commands behind menu entries that had none: `--open-ticket` writes the question the triage's own escalation names (`questionForEscalation` is keyed on `plan.escalation`, so a gate-removed finding gets a question about the gate rather than the "already tried and it did not move" wording, which would state an attempt that never happened), and it is **idempotent** — a live ticket for that exact step/volume/finding is named, not duplicated, so `autopilot.js` may call it every iteration; `--choose=<optionId> --ticket=<id> --reason=<text>` records the manager's pick (`decidedBy: "delivery-manager"`) and prints `npm run fix -- --ticket=<id>` when the option needs code, which is the door the dev team is summoned through; `--accept-patch` / `--reject-patch` judge a proposal. Each is one act at a time (two in one command is refused, exit 2), `--open-ticket` and `--choose` are allowed in both modes because a ticket and a choice are the manager's own words rather than an action on the corpus, and both are refused with `--no-write`. Exit 0 completed, 1 stopped short, 2 refused the request itself. Writes `postMortemDir()/delivery-plan.md` + `.json` (the manager's own report lives beside the step reports). No `AI_*` variable is read, and no model call is reachable from it. |
| `utils/patches.js` | **The patch channel** — the only place a code change becomes a record, and the rules that say what a change may touch (no model call). `BANNED_PATCH_PATHS` is the table: the guard tables themselves (`utils/{tickets,resume,delivery-verify,ledger,runlock,patches}.js`), the tests that pin them, `hooks/**`, `.env` / `.env.example`, the machine state (`.postmortem/`, `.logs/`, `.dry-run/`), the corpus (generated artifacts, `test-series/`, `.rejected` evidence, `-rolling-state.json`), and anything outside `ai-client/`. Each rule has an `id` and is evaluated **specific-first, catch-all-last**, so a refusal names WHICH rule it hit; a path that cannot be normalised to project-relative is banned, not allowed (`path-outside-project`). `normalizeProjectPath` resolves an absolute path against the root it was given (the ticket brief names the root in full, so an agent using the path it was handed must not be refused for it). `isProjectSourcePath` is a **whitelist** (`test/`, `utils/`, `configs/`, `system-prompts/`, `user-prompts/`, root-level `*.js`/`*.md`/`*.json`) because `ai-client/` also holds the run's own output — "allowed unless listed" would default to editing the evidence. `REQUIRED_CHECKS` pins `npm test` then `npm run pipeline-loop`; `runChecks` runs them through the shell (`npm` is a `.cmd` shim on Windows) with a 30-minute ceiling and stores `{id, command, exitCode, passed, tail, at}`; `normalizeChecks` takes the command from the table, never from the caller, so an unpinned id is reported MISSING rather than trusted. `readTestChain` + `testChainIsIntact` refuse a patch that removes a suite from `scripts.test` (the banned-path table cannot catch that: `package.json` is not a banned file). `PROPOSAL_CONTRACT` + `validateProposalShape` are fail-closed on the proposal: missing field / unusable path / banned path / a signal the acceptance test does not measure / a change the proposal does not name are **problems**; a thin summary, an outcome-only `verify`, a declared-but-unchanged file, a grown test chain, or touching a scripted-model answer key are **warnings**. `recordProposal` is the **only door** from a dev turn to a patch record and it attaches the proposal once; a refused attempt is stored in `refusedAttempts` (history) and does not stay in `problems` (the current state). `recordChecks` re-runs only for `proposed`/`verified`, and a failing re-verify moves a `verified` patch back to `proposed`. `judgeChecks` splits "never ran" from "ran and failed". `acceptPatch` / `rejectPatch` require a reason, and `judgmentReasonIsSound` refuses "volume 15 passes now" — a judgment about a patch is about what it changed, not about the complaint stopping. `pendingPatches` / `unresolvedPatches` are what act mode reads: an **unjudged patch is live code**, so act mode refuses the whole plan while one is open. `commitPatch` stages ONLY the patch's declared files (never `git add -A`), refuses unless the status is `accepted`, and refuses when the tree holds changes the patch does not declare; `revertPatch` restores tracked declared files and NAMES the files the patch created instead of deleting them (deleting is Tier C). `gitRootOf` asks git for the repository root instead of guessing from the folder layout, and `workingTreeChanges` scopes `git status --porcelain -uall` to that root — `-uall` because plain `--porcelain` collapses a brand-new folder into one entry, and both the "extra changes" list and `leftBehind` must name FILES. `renderPatchesMarkdown` / `renderProposalMarkdown` print what the manager can check, including the checks that never ran and the attempts the machine refused before this one. See §3.6 and gotcha 75. |
| `utils/devteam.js` | **The dev team** — the role that may change the code, and the turn that does it (one model call per patch). `patchFsTools({cwd, allowedDirs})` hands the agent the five file tools and an `approve` gate that refuses `deleteFile` outright and refuses every path `patchPathIsBanned` names, recording `{tool, path, rule, reason, at}` — the same two-layer shape as `utils/diagnostics.js`, so a write attempt is reported whichever layer stopped it, and `collectPatchWriteAttempts(refusals, toolCalls, advertised)` needs the `advertised` list to tell "the tool set never offered this" apart from "the gate refused this" (a write that SUCCEEDED belongs in `actualChanges`, the tree fingerprint, not in the refusals). `DEVTEAM_TOOLS_NOTE` is appended in code (AGENTS.md convention: prompt files stay mode-agnostic): the five tools, `oldString` not `oldText`, `grep`/`listFiles` take a FOLDER with `glob` as a filename ENDING, no `deleteFile`, the banned list with `ownerNote` as the route that does exist, the exact proposal JSON shape, and **"You have NO shell and no way to run a command. You cannot run `npm test`, and you must not claim you did."** — the harness gives an agent no shell, so the machine runs the checks. `devteamMaxStepsFor(readBytes)` scales the cap by what the turn must READ (`min(160, max(40, pages*3 + 32))`) — a dev turn reads code before it writes it, so it is allowed more steps than the auditor that only reads. `workTicket` is the whole turn in order: read the ticket (an unknown id refuses before any git or model call) → fingerprint the tree and read the test chain → **refuse if the tree already holds changes no patch declares** (before `createPatch`, so a refusal does not leave a dangling `proposed` patch gating act mode) → open the patch → `renderTicketForDev` → the turn → cross-check the declared files against what the tree actually changed → `recordProposal` as the only door. A turn that dies part-way is reported as an **unfinished patch**, not a clean refusal: the id, the files it changed, and `npm run fix -- --revert=<id>` are printed, because the code it wrote is already in the tree. `evidenceFootprint` resolves the ticket's evidence against the tree the turn actually edits, and `verifyPatch` / `describePatch` are the read-side. See §3.6 and gotcha 75. |
| `utils/manager.js` | **The delivery manager's own decision, as one tool-less call** — the only part of this layer that needs a model, and the only part where the "never sees the code" guarantee has to be a capability rather than a sentence. `MANAGER_ACTIONS` is the closed vocabulary of *decisions* (`run / diagnose / answer / choose / fix / judge / escalate / end`) — different from `DELIVERY_ACTIONS`, which is the vocabulary of pipeline *primitives* — and `ACTION_MENU_ENTRIES` maps each decision to the menu entry that gives it a command, so a decision the loop cannot carry out is caught by a test instead of discovered by a run. `MANAGER_RULES` is the standing contract (what it may read, what it may never read, what it may never do, its two limits), stated once per call so every decision in a run is made under one contract. `renderManagerBrief` builds the whole message from records this layer already wrote for a human — the triage, the deliverable counts, the remaining per-step attempts, each ticket via `renderTicketForManager`, each patch via `renderPatchForManager`, and the offered menu — and nothing else: no file is read, and the only place a source path reaches this role at all is a patch's declared file list. `parseManagerAction` is fail-closed (the LAST fenced block wins, for the same reason as the dev team's; a missing `reason` or a missing per-action field is not a decision). `validateManagerAction` is the gate: `run` must name the step on the offered menu; a ticket must exist; a diagnosis may not be asked twice (`--reask` is the account owner's move); an answer may only cite what `customerMayRead` allows and must say WHICH question when the ticket has more than one open; a chosen option must be one the filter allowed and must be the reason the dev team is called; a judgment must be about the change (`judgmentReasonIsSound`) and about a patch that is still unjudged; an escalation must be a sentence, not "idk"; `end` goes through `endIsProvable`. `endIsProvable` and `safeToAcceptAutomatically` are the two claims this module refuses to take on faith — the first against the records, the second against ordinary-code + green-checks + no-regression. `managerDecision` is `harness.runOneShot` with **no tools** (an agent handle WOULD be the code access this role is defined by not having), wrapped in `runTurnWithHooks(MANAGER_TASK, …)` so the role's own model switch fires around its own turn, and it is the reader of `DELIVERY_MAX_TOKENS`. A dead call names the wrong-container suspicion out loud. See §3.6 and gotcha 76. |
| `autopilot.js` | **The loop that drives a run**: read the state → open the ticket the triage says is the answer → offer the menu → ask the manager → type the command for its answer → re-read. It owns no gate, no budget, no wipe, no measurement and no record; every move is the account owner's own command spawned in its own process (`delivery.js --mode=act`, `diagnose.js --ticket=…`, `delivery.js --choose=…`, `fix.js --ticket=…`, `delivery.js --accept-patch=…` then `fix.js --commit=` / `--revert=`), which is both the reload boundary a code fix needs (gotcha 66) and the reason the loop cannot reach past a gate. `offerMoves` puts **exactly one `run` move** on the menu — the plan's entry point, labelled with the steps the same sequence then runs — because offering the later steps separately would let the manager skip the cascade. `commandFor` / `describeCommand` are one function, so watch mode prints the exact command act mode runs and the two cannot drift. `AUTOPILOT_MODE` defaults to **watch**: decide, print the decision + the whole menu + the command, and write nothing at all (no ticket, no ledger entry, no plan file, no lock) — the only safe rehearsal, since act mode refuses `--no-write` (gotcha 72). It takes **no run lock of its own** (its children take their own) but checks `runInProgress()` before each action and refuses to act beside a run. An accept is the one move it will not take on the manager's word: `safeToAcceptAutomatically` decides, and anything else stops and names the account owner. `AUTOPILOT_MAX_ITERATIONS` (default 12) is a wall, not a budget. No `--dry-run` — that flag also suppresses hooks, and the hooks decide which container answers (gotcha 22). See §3.6 and gotcha 76. |
| `diagnose.js` | **The diagnostics team's CLI** over `utils/diagnostics.js` + `utils/tickets.js`: `--ticket=<id>` answers one ticket, `--open` lists the tickets that have no answer yet, `--answer=<text>` (with optional `--question=<text>`) records the manager's reply to one of the team's clarifying questions, `--reask` allows a second diagnosis of a ticket that already has one, `--series=<dir>` (default `SERIES_LOCATION`), `--json`. An unknown flag is refused with exit 2. Re-asking without `--reask`, a closed or unknown ticket, and answering before a diagnosis exists are all refusals (exit 2) that happen **before any model call is reached**. It warns — deliberately does not refuse — when the run lock is held: reading a moving state is harmless for a role that cannot write, so the diagnosis is labelled a snapshot instead of being blocked. Exit 0 answered, 1 could not answer, 2 refused the request. |
| `fix.js` | **The dev team's CLI** over `utils/devteam.js` + `utils/patches.js` — the only route to a dev turn, and the only role in this layer that commits. `--open` lists tickets whose chosen option needs code and has no patch yet; `--status` lists the patches; `--show=<id>` prints one proposal as the manager reads it; `--ticket=<id>` runs the turn; `--verify=<id>` re-runs the pinned checks (and warns, without refusing, when the tree also holds changes the patch does not name — it mutates nothing); `--commit=<id>` and `--revert=<id>` land or undo a decided patch; `--series=<dir>`; `--json`. One action at a time; an unknown flag is refused before any model call; a series folder that does not exist is refused. The run lock is **binding for anything that mutates the tree** (`--ticket`, `--commit`, `--revert`) and read-only flags are allowed while a run is in progress — the same direction as `delivery.js --mode=act`. `--verify` looks the patch up BEFORE running the real test chain, so an unknown id exits 2 without spending minutes. Exit 0 done, 1 the proposal failed the contract or a check failed, 2 the request itself was refused. See §3.6 and gotcha 75. |
| `utils/runlock.js` | **The "a run is in progress" flag** — `<POSTMORTEM_DIR>/run.lock`, written by whoever is writing the volumes. `acquireRunLock({ by })` writes `{ run, pid, host, startedAt, by, nested }` and counts nesting (a step runner started inside a delivery action shares the parent's lock; only the writer deletes it). `runInProgress()` reads it and asks whether the pid is alive on this host; **a pid it cannot check (a lock written on a different machine) is reported as in progress**, because guessing is how an artifact ends half-built by one process and half by another. `describeRunLock` produces the sentence a refusal prints, including the lock file to delete when the run is genuinely gone. Advisory for the pipeline (`index.js` / every gulp task warn and continue if the lock cannot be written — a read-only checkout must not lose a run), **binding for the manager** (`delivery.js --mode=act` refuses if it cannot tell). The lock lives in `POSTMORTEM_DIR`, not per series, so two concurrent runs of two different series collide; the documented answer is a separate `POSTMORTEM_DIR` per series, and the refusal says so. See gotcha 72. |
| `utils/hooks.js` | Per-machine pipeline-hook runner (git-style, entirely optional). Discovers `hooks/pre-<task>` / `post-<task>` (and `pre-/post-pipeline`) and `exec`s each as an executable with `AI_CLIENT_*` env vars; skips when the file is absent, under `--dry-run`, or not executable. Applied via `withHooks()` in gulpfile.js. `SUPPORT_TASKS` (`manager`, `autopilot`) are **pseudo-steps**: hook names that are not gulp tasks and are deliberately kept out of `TASKS`, so `pre-manager` / `post-manager` / `pre-autopilot` / `post-autopilot` exist without pretending the support roles are pipeline stages. `runTurnWithHooks(task, turnFn)` wraps a call in a task's hooks at CALL time — the right shape for a CLI or a role module that is already running, where `withHooks` (which wraps at definition time) is the right shape for a gulp task. The guarantee is that each support role fires the hooks around its OWN model turn: `utils/diagnostics.js`, `utils/devteam.js` and `utils/manager.js` do, `diagnose.js` and `fix.js` fire none (a CLI cannot know whether its module is about to reach the model without repeating every refusal the module makes, and a container switch costs a model load). See §3 "Pipeline hooks" and `hooks/README.md`. |
| `utils/disputes.js` | The glossary disputes queue — the one channel that carries terminology findings BACKWARDS (deterministic, no AI). The verifier reads the source, and sometimes what it finds is that the GLOSSARY is wrong rather than the translation. `parseGlossaryDisputes` reads the `GLOSSARY DISPUTE:` blocks out of a verifier's reply (term / canonical / proposed / the two quotes); `mergeDisputes` aggregates them by source term across volumes (the same term challenged twice is one dispute with two pieces of evidence, and a report with no evidence never overwrites one that has it); `saveGlossaryDisputes` writes `glossary-disputes.json` + `glossary-disputes.md` at the series root. `verify-translate` records them per chapter and writes the queue; `glossary.js` reads the queue as a first-class input to its amend pass (`buildDisputesNote`); `loadVolumeReferences` reads it and `chapterTerminology` marks a disputed term in the prompt — still required, but named as provisional, so the QA loop cannot argue with itself. A dispute with no source quote is recorded and flagged `unsupported` (a complaint, not a correction). |
| `utils/qa-loop.js` | Shared QA-loop orchestration: `runSharedQaLoop(cfg)` runs the whole-installment validator → grader → feedback loop for all four volume tasks (glossary, character-voice, style-guide, jump-in-wiki). It centralizes the rolling window, the fail-closed unparseable-score handling, the per-iteration state persistence (with the source fingerprint), the acceptance-criterion check, the **passing-grade re-grade path** (`confirmPassingScore` — see §3 "QA loop"), the **no-op feedback stop** (it fingerprints the artifacts `cfg.feedbackArtifactFiles` names before and after `runFeedback`, and ends the loop when they are byte-identical instead of paying for another validator turn and another grade over an unchanged document), the validator recovery-turn gating, and the `ON_QA_LIMIT` policy — each task injects only its validator creation, turn prompt + labels, recovery prompt, acceptance check, feedback stage, feedback artifact list, and log lines. Also exports `confirmExceptionalScore` — the exceptional-grade fast-accept path, which the four CHUNKED loops call directly (they keep their own inline loop, because their per-segment validators + findings-merge stage do not fit the single-validator interface, but they must run the SAME confirmation or the biggest volumes would be the only ones that never got it). Also exports `runVolumeWithModeFallback` — the whole-installment → chapter-by-chapter retry: it runs a volume's processing pass, and ONLY when that pass throws `isTooBigForOnePassError` does it wipe the attempt's outputs (`wipeAttemptOutputs`, including the rolling-state file, so the retry cannot inherit half a document or scores recorded for documents that no longer exist), flip `ctx.chunked`, and run the volume again — exactly once. A hang, a malformed tool call, a dead container, a missing previous artifact, a structural error or a bad ACCEPTANCE score do NOT trigger it (see gotcha 55). |
| `harness.js` | The AI layer: one-shot calls, agent handles, wiki tools, gated fs tools (**five tools: readFile / listFiles / grep / writeFile / editFile — `deleteFile` is not offered, and the gate refuses it anyway**, gotcha 8; with the read caps raised from the library defaults — `AGENT_MAX_LINE_LENGTH` / `AGENT_MAX_READ_BYTES`, see §3 and gotcha 58 — and the **file-tool contract** applied to grep/listFiles/readFile: a folder where a model wrote a file, a filename ending where it wrote a wildcard, and an archive refused as text; see gotcha 60), **epub tools (`createEpubTools`: `epubInfo` / `readEpubText` / `stageVolume` + its own approve gate — the intake agent's senses, so it can open a book instead of guessing from its name; text comes back in bounded windows, staging is confined to the allowed dirs, never clobbers a different file, and LINKS the book into the volume folder rather than duplicating it — see gotcha 77)**, provider plumbing, run logging, the runaway-generation guard (aborts agent turns that produce excessive text without tool calls), **the truncation guard** (an agent turn that ends with `finish_reason=length` while its last step was a `writeFile`/`editFile` throws — a file written against the output-token cap is incomplete and would otherwise pass `assertWrote`; every `length` finish logs a greppable WARNING **through `logLine`, so it reaches `summary.log` and not only stderr** — gotcha 64), and the idle call deadline (`AI_CALL_DEADLINE_MS`, default 60 min — aborts a model call that makes no progress for that long; all fetch timeouts are disabled, so this is the only wall-clock bound on a call). Never bypass it to talk to the model. Multi-model support: per-call `endpoint` override (baseUrl/apiKey/model, falling back to `AI_*`), per-call `temperature` + sampling params (`topP/topK/minP/repetitionPenalty/presencePenalty`), the `hy-mt` thinking dialect (`no_think|low|high` → `reasoning_effort`), nullable `systemPrompt` (Hy-MT2's single-user-message contract), and `assertModelServing()` — a `GET /v1/models` control-plane check that fails loudly before a stage's first call (and logs the raw model list it got) — plus `measurePromptTokens()` — the token-calibration probe: one user message, `max_tokens: 1`, no system prompt, no thinking, and the server's own `usage.prompt_tokens` is the answer (an OpenAI-compatible server reports the prompt count only as part of a completion, and this machine's server has no `/tokenize` endpoint — probed: `/tokenize`, `/v1/tokenize`, `/api/tokenize`, `/num_tokens` all 404). `tagSizeOverflowError()` recognizes the server's own "prompt + max tokens exceeds the context" phrasings and tags the error as `tooBigForOnePassError`, so a task above can act on a SIZE failure without string-matching a server's error text (the two truncation guards above are tagged the same way). Logs every AI call to `.logs/<timestamp>/` — per-agent chat histories (system prompt, messages, assistant response, reasoning, tool calls) and one-shot call dumps — plus the summary log (greppable `CALL`/`RESULT`/`WARNING`/`[calibrate]` lines). |
| `research.js` | Client-side web research (Wikipedia Action API + optional Brave/Tavily/Serper). No LLM involved. |
| `glossary.js` | Glossary task logic. Also the far end of the disputes channel: it reads the series `glossary-disputes.json` and hands the open disputes to the amend pass as a first-class input (`buildDisputesNote` → the `{{DISPUTES}}` section of `user-prompts/glossary.md`), where each one must be settled — correct the entry, or record in its Notes column the evidence that makes the canonical rendering stand. The glossary validator checks that every listed dispute was decided. And the home of the cumulative-document rules (gotcha 64): `seedGlossaryFromPrevious` (copy the previous volume's glossary into this volume's folder before any agent runs), `buildGlossaryIndex` + `glossaryIndexBlock` (the compact term → rendering map inlined into the turn), `glossaryWriteInstruction` + `glossaryRecoveryPrompt` (edit in place with `editFile`; the whole-file `writeFile` is only for a file that does not exist yet), `compareGlossaryCarryForward` / `assertGlossaryCarryForward` / `guardCarryForwardAgainst` / `quarantineDamagedGlossary` / `reportCarryForwardLoss` (the no-AI gate that fails a volume whose glossary lost carried-forward terms, and moves the damaged file to `glossary.md.rejected` so the next volume cannot read it; it reports three legitimate forms of carrying an entry forward — unchanged, `restructured` (widened or split), and `renamed`, see gotcha 68), `glossaryTermSpans` (the alias spellings inside ONE row's term column — the gate compares entries at THAT resolution, because widening a row with a new alias is an improvement and not a loss; see gotcha 65), `glossaryRowText` + `normalizeGlossaryRendering` (the row-wide and rendering-side evidence the rename test uses), and `parseGlossaryTableTerms` (now also returns each row's existing `rendering` and its `notes`, which is what the index, a conflict check and the rename test need). |
| `character-voice.js` | Character voice reference task logic — extracts speech quirks, POV markers, narration types, and produces a cumulative character voice reference and per-volume POV maps. Carries the same cumulative-document rules glossary.js established (gotcha 64): `seedVoiceReferenceFromPrevious` (copy the previous volume's `character-voice.md` in before any agent runs — only that file, because `pov-map.md` is per-volume and a stale copy of it would be worse than none), `parseVoiceSections` + `voicePrimaryName` (the `### Character` sections, each keyed on the part of its heading that says WHO it is about, so a reworded persona tag is not read as a deleted character), `buildVoiceIndex` + `voiceIndexBlock`, `voiceWriteInstruction` + `voiceRecoveryPrompt` (amend the reference in place with `editFile`; the POV map is still written whole), `compareVoiceCarryForward` / `assertVoiceCarryForward` / `guardVoiceCarryForwardAgainst` (the no-AI gate on the character-section set, run between chapters too), and `voiceAuthorMaxSteps` (the scaled cap — this stage carried a flat 30 until gotcha 65). |
| `style-guide.js` | Style guide task logic — extracts style-relevant constructs (honorifics, pronouns, particles, internal-monologue markers, onomatopoeia, POV/scene markers, tense, punctuation, wordplay) and produces a cumulative style guide of rendering policies for the target language. Same cumulative-document rules: `seedStyleGuideFromPrevious`, `parseStyleSections` / `countStyleRules` / `buildStyleIndex` / `styleIndexBlock`, `styleWriteInstruction` / `styleRecoveryPrompt`, `compareStyleCarryForward` / `assertStyleCarryForward` / `guardStyleCarryForwardAgainst`, `styleAuthorMaxSteps`. Its carry-forward gate is deliberately narrower than the other two: it checks the `##` category set the prompt specifies (a guide missing "Address & Honorifics" has lost everything inside it) and REPORTS a drop in the rule count instead of failing on it — a guide that says the same thing in fewer words is not damaged, and a guard that compares free prose starts calling improvements losses (gotcha 65). |
| `jump-in-wiki.js` | Wiki task logic **plus the shared helpers**. The wiki builds cumulatively on the previous volume's `wiki.md` + `shared-wiki.md` (looked up in the manifest's reading order, never by `N-1` folder arithmetic) — a missing/stub previous wiki is governed by `ON_MISSING_PREVIOUS` like the other three cumulative tasks (abort, or warn-and-skip with `skip`). After all volumes: the last existing `shared-wiki.md` is copied to the series artifacts directory (`SERIES_ARTIFACTS_DIR`, default `<SERIES_LOCATION>/shared-wiki.md`); writes the per-volume translation handoff (`utils/handoff.js`) on both paths. |
| `consistency-audit.js` | Final cross-artifact consistency audit (the pre-translation sign-off). An audit agent (gated fs tools, cwd = series root, **writes confined to `consistency-report.md` alone** — the four artifacts are genuinely read-only: a wider gate would let the agent "fix" an artifact by overwriting the very thing it is judging) reads the four series-root artifacts (`glossary.md`, `character-voice.md`, `style-guide.md`, `shared-wiki.md`) and writes `consistency-report.md` (PASS/FAIL verdict + severity-banded findings with quoted snippets). The four artifacts are hashed before the agent runs and re-hashed after; if any changed in flight the audit is void (a structural error — the report signs off a state that no longer exists on disk). No QA loop. Idempotent: content-based — the report also writes a `consistency-report.md.provenance.json` sidecar carrying the sha256 fingerprint of each of the four audited artifacts; the report is skipped while all four fingerprints still match the current files (regenerating any artifact invalidates it). A missing or corrupt sidecar falls back to the legacy mtime check (report newer than all four artifacts). `--force` re-audits. A FAIL verdict is logged loudly but does not fail the task — the report is the deliverable. The `verify-translate` borderline tiebreak (the `AUDIT_*` endpoint re-scoring chapters near the passing line) keeps the auditor's findings when the auditor is the pessimist, so a retranslate of a tiebreak-flipped chapter fixes what the auditor flagged. |
| `translate.js` | Translation task (first stage of the multi-model chain; §8.5). Per volume, per chapter (in `bundle.segments` order): skip when the draft + `translation-state.json` cover the current source/reference hashes, size each chapter's parts with `planChapterSplit` (tokens — see §3 "Processing mode" and gotcha 57; `TRANSLATE_CHUNK_CHARS` is a ceiling only when explicitly set), translate each part via `runOneShot` on the `TRANSLATE_*` endpoint — **no system prompt** (Hy-MT2's single-user-message contract), official sampling, `no_think` by default — with the previous part's ending as continuity context, deterministic QA (`checkTranslationQa`), per-chapter state persistence, and the merged `translation.md` + `translation-qa.md`. **Entry gate:** `checkTranslationPreconditions` refuses to start the stage on a FAIL (or missing, or stale) `consistency-report.md`, or on a volume with no `glossary.md` — `--allow-fail` / `--allow-no-glossary` override. **A chapter that is empty IN THE SOURCE is skipped without a model call** (under `TRANSLATION_EMPTY_SOURCE_CHARS`, default 40) and recorded as "empty in source — not translated"; a segment the extraction flagged `empty` but that still has text is translated with a loud warning (a damaged chapter, not a missing one). **A deterministic-QA failure no longer discards the draft:** the bad draft is kept, the rejected attempt is quarantined to `translation-<id>.rejected.md`, and the state records `qaFailed` + `qaFindings` so `verify` can seed the verdict with NO model call and `retranslate` can repair it. **Completeness is checked at the END of the task** (a volume with chapters that have no text is a structural error, but chapters missing *because they are empty in the source* are a reported warning — one hole in volume 2 no longer stops volumes 3–17). Also exports `chapterArtifactNames` / `mergeVolumeTranslationFiles` shared by the other three tasks; `mergeVolumeTranslationFiles` returns `{text, missing, unverified}` and injects the UNVERIFIED marker (see §8.5). |
| `verify-translate.js` | Verification task (§8.5). Per chapter with a draft: one-shot source-anchored check on the `VERIFY_*` endpoint → 0–100 score (fail-closed: unparseable = FAIL) + severity-banded findings → `translation-verification.json` sidecar + `translation-verification.md` report. PASS = score ≥ `PASSING_SCORE` (default 70). **Borderline tiebreak (batched, cross-model):** after the verify pass, every chapter whose score lands within `±VERIFY_TIEBREAK_BAND` (default 5) of the passing score is re-scored on the `AUDIT_*` endpoint (a SECOND endpoint — the `verify-audit` hook switches its container in) and the two scores are **averaged** (one endpoint switch for the whole batch, never interleaved with the verify loop; an unparseable audit score keeps the verifier's score — fail-open). **Repeat sampling:** a chapter whose score lands within `±VERIFY_SAMPLE_BAND` (default 8) of the passing line is graded up to `VERIFY_SAMPLES` (default 2, max 5) times and the MEDIAN is used — one grader's bad hour is not a FAIL. **Deterministic seed:** a chapter the translate task already failed on the no-AI QA checks gets its verdict from that record (no model call) so the loop can repair it. **Rendering-variant scan** (`findRenderingVariants`, no model call at all): every glossary term used in the volume is checked against the PUBLISHED text for near-variants of its canonical rendering — a second rendering of the same source term (HIGH), different spacing/hyphenation (MEDIUM), different capitalisation or a plural alongside the singular (LOW). The per-chapter verifier reads one chapter at a time and cannot see this class at all; across the volume it is free. Findings go in `translation-verification.md` + the sidecar's `volume.renderingVariants`. **Cross-chapter consistency pass** (`runVolumeConsistencyPass`, default-ON, `VOLUME_CONSISTENCY_ENABLED`): the same audit batch also reads a volume's PUBLISHED chapters TOGETHER — the one thing a per-chapter check structurally cannot see (a name rendered two ways inside the volume, a fact chapter 3 states and chapter 9 denies, a tense/POV shift, a contradiction with the previous volume's ending). One call per audit WINDOW (a volume larger than the auditor's context is split into consecutive windows, each carrying the previous window's tail, and the report says which chapters were never compared); findings are chapter-tagged (`FINDING [HIGH] chapters=ch3,ch9 — …` with both sides quoted) into `volume-consistency.json` + `volume-consistency.md`, and a HIGH finding makes even a 92-scoring chapter a retranslate target. **Glossary disputes:** a verifier reply may raise a `GLOSSARY DISPUTE:` block (the translation was right, the glossary was not); those are recorded per chapter and aggregated into the series-root disputes queue at the end of the run. `VERIFY_TRANSLATE_ENABLED=false` makes it a no-op.
