That is wrong here: the translation stage runs up to four different models (translate / verify / edit / audit), each with its own tokenizer, and on this machine they all share one port and one model alias (`local`, gotcha 22). A number stamped by the intake agent is a lie the moment the auditor budgets a prompt with it. So the split is: `scriptMix` (CJK characters vs everything else) is a property of the TEXT and is persisted in the bundle cache (`BUNDLE_SCHEMA_VERSION` 6, whole + per chapter, so a cached extraction never has to be re-read); the COEFFICIENTS are a property of the MODEL and are measured per endpoint at stage start, where the hooks have already switched the right container in. Measured, not assumed: a whole Japanese light novel is **0.588–0.622 tokens per character** (128,744–176,201 chars → 75,757–109,628 tokens across the 17 volumes), and the old coefficients (1.0 per CJK char, 0.35 per other) over-counted by **1.42–1.47× on both Japanese AND Latin text** — which is why the prompt budget threw away roughly a fifth of the reference material it could have kept. Consequences: (a) `estimateTokens` must NOT bill the 52-token chat template, because `fitPromptBudget` estimates per BLOCK and would charge the same overhead five times — it is subtracted once, in `tokenBudgetFor`; (b) the calibration key `baseUrl|model` cannot distinguish two containers that advertise the same alias, which is why `TOKEN_CALIBRATION_MAX_AGE_HOURS` exists — the age is what eventually re-measures a swapped model; (c) a probe is a real request (this server has no `/tokenize` — `/tokenize`, `/v1/tokenize`, `/api/tokenize`, `/num_tokens` all 404, and `max_tokens: 0` is IGNORED, so a probe must use `max_tokens: 1`), which is why the pipeline calibrates the model once and derives every volume's count from character counts instead of probing each volume; (d) calibration is FAIL-SOFT — a probe that cannot run leaves the built-in coefficients, which over-count, so token accounting can never be the reason a run dies.

55. **The whole→chunked fallback triggers on ONE tagged error class, and a bad acceptance score is deliberately not it.** A whole-installment pass is the cheap, cache-friendly path, so when it turns out not to fit, retrying the same volume chapter by chapter is a genuine repair rather than a retry of the same mistake. But "on any error, try chunked" is a trap: a hang, a malformed tool call, a dead container and a missing previous artifact are not size problems, and chunking them costs the whole volume again to fix something chunking cannot fix. So `tooBigForOnePassError` (the mirror of `structuralError` in `configs/shared.js`) is tagged at exactly three sites in `harness.js` — the server refusing a request because prompt + `max_tokens` exceed its context (`tagSizeOverflowError` matches the phrasings, so no task has to string-match a server's error text), an agent turn truncated while writing a file, and a tool-less call that hit the output cap — and `runVolumeWithModeFallback` acts on nothing else. **A low ACCEPTANCE score is not a size failure and must never auto-switch the mode**: it is the QA loop's problem, it is stochastic, and switching on it would make which mode ran — and therefore what the artifacts look like — irreproducible between runs. The fallback also wipes the failed attempt's outputs AND its `*-rolling-state.json` before retrying, because the two modes leave different files behind and the chunked path would otherwise read a partial artifact, a research skeleton full of `- (pending)` lines, and scores recorded for documents that no longer exist (gotcha 3, gotcha 50). It runs ONCE: a second failure reports both attempts rather than looping. And a whole-mode failure is worth READING even when it recovers — the size check said the volume fitted, so either the estimate is off for that model or the cumulative reference outgrew the allowance; the fallback message says so and names `SOURCE_CHUNK_SAFETY_FRACTION`.

56. **A whole-mode pass carries the whole book in the agent's own session, and the request-window arithmetic cannot see that.** In whole mode the author agent reads the entire volume through one `readFile` call (OpenHarness's `NodeFsProvider` default cap is 10 MB, so a 150 KB book arrives in one call — roughly 100k tokens), and that text then sits in its session alongside the system prompt, the previous volume's cumulative reference, and the file it is writing. The session auto-compacts at `AI_CONTEXT_WINDOW`, and compaction is LOSSY: it is how "the agent quietly stopped honoring the honorific rules" first shows up — as a worse artifact, not as a failure. `planProcessingMode`'s fraction check bounds the REQUEST; it cannot bound the session. That is why the decision also prints a warning when the whole volume alone exceeds half the window, and why the honest reading of a whole-mode run is the artifact quality, not just the exit code. If a volume trips it repeatedly, `--chunked` is the correct answer for that volume — not a bigger window.

57. **A character constant cannot see the answer, so it split half the series for no reason.** `TRANSLATE_CHUNK_CHARS = 24000` was the last size decision in the pipeline still measured in characters, and it applied identically whether the model had a 262,144-token window or a 32,000 one, whether thinking was on or off, and whether the chapter was Japanese or Latin. Measured against the real 17 volumes: it split **55 of 133 chapters**, while the largest chapter (49,840 characters = 34,716 tokens) leaves 161,840 tokens of the window free for the references after its output reserve — one call, no seam. The token plan splits **0 of 133** under the normal settings and **30 of 133** when `TRANSLATE_THINKING=high`, because reasoning tokens are billed as output and must fit the same cap as the translation; the character rule would have split the same 55 either way, which is the tell that it was not measuring anything. Two consequences for anyone changing this: (a) the **output cap is subtracted twice on purpose** — once in the admission limit (the server adds `max_tokens` to the prompt when it decides whether to accept the request, gotcha 36, so it is gone from the window whether or not the model uses it) and once as the ceiling on the expected answer (the generation is cut off mid-chapter if the answer exceeds it) — and (b) the conversion from tokens to characters uses the chapter's OWN measured characters-per-token, never a per-language constant, because the same token allowance is worth ~2.8× more Latin characters than CJK ones (gotcha 54). `TRANSLATE_CHUNK_CHARS` is still honoured as a hard ceiling when an operator sets it explicitly — arithmetic does not overrule a deliberate limit — and `translateChunkCap()` returning `null` is what distinguishes "unset" from "the default".

58. **A chat reply is not evidence of an artifact, and one reply is never two documents.** `assertWroteWithFallback` recovers an agent that produced its output in its chat reply instead of calling `writeFile` by writing that reply to disk. Unconditional, that is a corruption path, and it was observed live end to end: an author agent could not read the previous volume's character-voice reference (the file tools cut every line longer than 2000 characters, and a compiled reference holds one long line per character — 2,344 at volume 02, 2,863 at volume 03), so it tried to split the long lines to see them, never restored them, ran out of turn budget, and its final reply was the sentence *"I need the full content of two very long lines in the previous reference. Let me temporarily split them…"*. The fallback wrote that 164-character sentence into **both** expected outputs (`character-voice.md` and `pov-map.md`). 164 characters of English prose is neither empty nor a `(stub —` marker, so it passed `assertRealOutput`, the acceptance loop, the validation report, and the last-good-volume copy walk — and became the series root's character voice reference. Three rules now stand between a no-op turn and a published artifact, and they are all load-bearing:
    - **`looksLikeArtifact`** (utils/fs.js): the reply must be at least `AGENT_RECOVERY_MIN_CHARS` (1000) characters AND hold a Markdown heading or a table — the shape every prompt in this pipeline already specifies. Size alone is not evidence, and neither is confidence.
    - **One reply, one file.** The fallback writes only when exactly ONE expected output is missing. A two-file gap is repaired by the recovery turn, never by duplicating one reply across both artifacts.
    - **Refusing is not fatal.** The call still returns `true`, so the caller sends the recovery turn, and `assertRealOutput` after it is the hard stop. The asymmetry to remember: a missing file fails loudly and is picked up on a re-run, while a plausible-looking wrong file gets audited, published, copied to the series root, and injected into the next volume's prompts. Consequence: with `AGENT_RECOVERY_ENABLED=false`, a two-file gap now FAILS the volume instead of publishing a pair of wrong artifacts — turning recovery off means the hard stop is the only thing left.
    The other half of the fix is the read caps (`AGENT_MAX_LINE_LENGTH` 8000 / `AGENT_MAX_READ_BYTES` 65536 in `createGatedFsTools`): an agent that can read the artifact it is required to preserve does not start reconstructing it from memory. Raising the byte cap is also a token fix — reading a 150 KB artifact in five paged calls re-bills the growing transcript five times, which is a large part of why the character-voice stage cost 173M tokens for five volumes. Do not "improve" this by writing the reply anyway when the gate refuses: the gate exists because the pipeline cannot tell a document from a sentence about a plan by checking that the file is non-empty.
    - **The root copy is where it would have spread.** The four series-level copies walk the volume list backwards and publish the first hit, and the audit, every later volume's prompts and the translation stage read that copy. They now use `isPublishableArtifact` (gotcha 50): real AND the shape of the document it claims to be, warned about rather than skipped silently. The per-volume SKIP checks deliberately keep the looser `hasRealOutput` — a stricter skip rule only makes volumes regenerate forever, while the copy rule is where one bad artifact actually reaches the series.

59. **The reasoning budget is a shared resource, and an empty reply is what starvation looks like.** Measured on the live 17-volume run: 203,961,788 input tokens, 173M of them in the character-voice stage, 22 calls that answered with nothing, and one glossary call that spent 131,072 reasoning tokens and produced **0 characters** of content (`finish_reason=length`). A reasoning phase that consumes the whole reply budget does not produce a worse answer, it produces no answer — and the pipeline's guards can only report that. The rule that keeps it bounded: **a call whose job is to produce a number samples like a grader** — `JUDGE_TEMPERATURE` plus `STAGE_THINKING_LEVEL` (default `medium`), through `judgeThinking()` (configs/shared.js) for the calls outside the translation stage and `stageThinking()` (which now delegates to it) inside. The four pre-production acceptance graders used to inherit the AUTHORING level (`AI_THINKING_LEVEL`, default `xhigh`) because `stageThinking` never reached them — that is the shape the 22 empty replies came out of. What still runs at `AI_THINKING_LEVEL`, deliberately: the authoring agent turns (author / validator / feedback / intake / audit agent) and the extraction one-shots, because an agent writing a 150 KB artifact is the call that needs the thinking, and `AI_THINKING_LEVEL` is the per-series knob for it (gotcha 1). The tell when a run is starving: `reasoning=` next to `content=` in `.logs/*/summary.log` — a call whose reasoning dwarfs its content is the one to turn down. The prompt audit's `judging-dialect` rule reports any grading call that samples like a writing call, so the gap cannot come back quietly.

60. **The file tools are stricter than the way a model reaches for them, and the schema cannot catch it.** `dirPath` is `z.string()` and `glob` is `z.string()`: a file where a folder belongs, or a wildcard where a suffix belongs, passes validation and only fails (or silently succeeds at nothing) when the tool runs. Measured on the live series' logged runs — 1030 grep calls: **40 died with `ENOTDIR: not a directory`** and **398 searched for nothing and reported "No matches found"**. Three separate mismatches, all agent-facing:
    - **`grep`/`listFiles` take a FOLDER, and the tool walks it.** The pipeline's own prompts caused the crash: `glossary.js` names one file and says "read it selectively with readFile/grep", so the agent passes that file as `dirPath`. `readFile` takes a file; `grep` does not. Recoverable (the model sees the error and retries) but every one is a wasted step out of a validator's capped budget.
    - **`grep`'s `glob` is a filename ENDING** — the library's implementation is `file.endsWith(glob)`, and its own test uses `glob: ".ts"` — but the word "glob" makes every model write `"*.md"`. That matches NOTHING and answers `No matches found for /*.md/.` **This is the worse half**: it is silent, and an agent that searched `*whole.md` and got nothing reports the term as absent from the source. A wrong artifact, not a wasted call.
    - **The library's binary-file list does not know `.epub`.** A grep over a volume folder (which holds the staged book) or the series root (which holds all 17) decoded zip bytes as UTF-8 and matched the noise; `readFile` on a staged book answered mojibake. The intake brief already promised "the file tools REFUSE .epub paths" — it was enforced only for `filePath`, so `grep(dirPath: "book.epub")` walked straight through it.

    The fix is three-layered, and all three layers matter: the **tool descriptions** state the contract (the description is the half the model reads), the **input is repaired** and the repair is reported in the answer's `status` line so the agent learns instead of being quietly redirected (`applyFsToolContract` + `normalizeDirToolInput` in harness.js), and the **fs provider refuses an archive as text** (`withBookAwareReads`) — which is also what the library's grep wants, since it catches a failed read and skips the file. Two invariants to keep: the normalization runs INSIDE `wrapToolsWithApproval`, so the approve gate always judges the agent's ORIGINAL path (a repair can never smuggle a denied path through); and `editFile`'s parameter is `oldString`, not `oldText` — the prompts used to say `oldText`, and a model that obeys the prompt instead of the schema gets `Invalid input for tool editFile`. Pinned in `test/test-artifact-integrity.js` **both ways** (the raw library still crashes on a file, still matches nothing for `*.md`, still reads an epub as text; the tools an agent is handed do not) plus `test/test-intake.js` for the gate at `dirPath`. Related: a regex from another dialect (`(?i)vice`) used to throw a raw `SyntaxError` at the agent; it is now answered with the flag the tool actually has (`ignoreCase`).

61. **An agent transcript without tool results is a transcript that cannot explain itself.** `consumeEvents` handled `tool.start` and `tool.error` but had no case for the library's `tool.done`, so 526 logged agent turns recorded every tool CALL and none of their answers — while AGENTS.md promised "tool calls + results". `escapeInline` was the other half: it ran `String()` on the tool input, which answers `[object Object]`, so even the calls were unreadable in the chat log (only `summary.log`, which uses `summarizeInput`, showed the real arguments). The distinction that was being lost is the one this pipeline most needs: "the search ran and found nothing" versus "the search never ran" (see gotcha 60) look identical in a log that records neither. Pairing is by `toolCallId`, not "the last call seen" — a step fires several calls at once, and an error used to be pinned onto a call that had succeeded.

62. **A failed model call reached `consumeEvents` as an event, and nothing looked at it.** Found by running the real task against the scripted endpoint (`test/fake-backend.js`), not by a live run. When the endpoint fails — a 500, a refused request, a provider error — the AI SDK does not throw into the `for await`: it emits an `error` part, and openharness forwards it as an `error` event and then closes the turn (`turn.start` … `error` … `turn.done`) WITHOUT ever emitting `done`. So `result.result` stayed `null`, `consumeEvents` only threw on `result.result === "error"`, `result.error` was carried and discarded, and `runOneShot` reported **"The model returned no content"** for every failure. Three consequences, all real:
   - `tagSizeOverflowError` never saw the server's own "prompt (4337 tokens) + max tokens (262144) exceeds the context" wording, so `tooBigForOnePassError` was never tagged on the one-shot path and **`runVolumeWithModeFallback` never fired for the single failure it exists to repair** (gotcha 55). The whole-installment pass simply failed, and the log blamed the model.
   - An overnight run's log said the model answered with nothing when the container was not running. The actionable sentence was in `result.error` and was thrown away.
   - The non-streaming fallback ran for a request the server had already refused for being too large — a second doomed request, at the same size, guaranteed to fail the same way.

   `consumeEvents` now throws `result.error` whenever the stream ended without a normal completion (`complete` / `max_steps`), and `runOneShot` skips the non-streaming retry when the error is already tagged as a size failure. The rule to keep: **an event loop that records an error must also surface it** — a `result` object with an `error` field nobody reads is how a pipeline reports a healthy model for a dead one. Pinned in `test/test-fake-backend.js` (the `tooBig` / `serverError` / `hang` scenarios, plus the scenario that hands the real tagged error to `runVolumeWithModeFallback` and asserts the volume actually re-runs chapter by chapter — the half `test-tokens.js` cannot reach, because there the error is hand-built) and in `test/test-translate-stage.js` (the `dead` scenario, which asserts the run log carries the server's message and specifically does NOT contain "returned no content").

63. **A scripted model server that lives in the test process deadlocks with a synchronous child spawn.** `test/test-pipeline-loop.js` has to run each task in its own process (task modules read `SERIES_LOCATION` at module load) while the scripted endpoint it talks to is an HTTP server in the PARENT process. `spawnSync` parks the parent's event loop for the whole life of the child, so the server accepts the connection and then never processes a single request. Every stage hangs until `AI_CALL_DEADLINE_MS` fires and the harness reports "the agent turn made no progress for 2 min and was aborted … the endpoint is likely hung or the model container died" — a message that blames an endpoint which was, in fact, blocked by the test's own runner. The tell that separates the two: **a hang with zero requests recorded**. A real dead endpoint still gets the request; a blocked server never receives one. The fix is to await an asynchronous `spawn` (`runStageAsync`). The rule to keep: anything that must answer a request while another process runs has to be awaited, never blocked on — and when a stub-driven run reports a hung endpoint, check the request count before believing it.

64. **A cumulative document eventually outgrows one reply, and a prompt that asks a model to reproduce it destroys the part it could not reach.** Every glossary pass used to end with "write the complete glossary to `glossary.md` using `writeFile` (complete contents, overwrite)". That is right for volume 01 and impossible from volume 03 onward: the output cap is a quarter of `AI_CONTEXT_WINDOW` (`envMaxTokens`, so 65,536 here), and the cumulative glossary measured 16 KB at volume 01, 45 KB at volume 02, **230 KB at volume 03, 268 KB at volume 04, 474 KB at volume 05**. Measured on the live 17-volume run: 25 step-cap warnings, all in the glossary stage, all on volume 03 or later (17 amend turns, 7 feedback turns, 1 findings merge); one turn's `writeFile` JSON argument was cut off **mid-string** and the tool call failed outright, killing volume 04 (its folder has a `glossary.md` and nothing else — no validation report, no coverage audit, no rolling state); the capped turns ended `finish_reason=tool-calls` still mid-work after 37–64 tool calls, 8–17 paged `readFile` calls and 16–39 `editFile` patches; `glossary-amend` alone cost 234M of the run's 465M tokens (~7.8M prompt tokens per call, worst single turn 14.1M). The failure class, not the model, is the thing to fix — **raising `maxSteps` only buys more paging and more tokens.** Four rules now stand together:
   - **The workflow copies the baseline in** (`seedGlossaryFromPrevious`) before any agent touches the volume folder. "Carry forward every existing term" was never a job for a model; it is a file copy. A re-run also RESETS a half-written glossary to the previous volume's clean baseline, which is what "carry forward" means.
   - **The instruction follows the file** (`glossaryWriteInstruction(hasExistingFile, mode)`, one implementation for the amend, feedback and per-chapter passes, and the same rule in `glossaryRecoveryPrompt`): an existing file is amended **in place with `editFile`**, and the whole-file `writeFile` is explicitly forbidden. Only the first volume (or a failed seed) is told to write it whole. The recovery turn used to demand the exact instruction that broke the file — it now edits.
   - **The agent gets a map, not a hunt** (`buildGlossaryIndex` → `glossaryIndexBlock`): every section with its terms and existing renderings, inlined, so placement and conflict checks need no paging. Capped by `GLOSSARY_INDEX_MAX_CHARS` (30000) and it **says** when it truncates (gotcha 43).
   - **A deterministic gate checks the invariant** (`compareGlossaryCarryForward` / `assertGlossaryCarryForward`, no model call). Nothing else in the stage can see a lost term — the validator reads this volume's source and the current glossary, and its own prompt tells it "you do not have the earlier volumes' sources". Observed: **volume 06 shipped 411 of volume 05's 769 terms** (59% gone) and volume 03 silently lost 8 while being accepted at 78/100. The gate fails the VOLUME and moves the damaged file to `glossary.md.rejected`, because a failed cumulative volume normally stops the next one only when its artifact is MISSING — a present-but-short one is the case `ON_MISSING_PREVIOUS` cannot see, and it would have been read as terminology law by every later volume. The chunked flow runs the gate **between chapters** (that is where volume 06's terms actually disappeared, and catching it at chapter 3 saves eight chapters of work on a broken base). Knob: `GLOSSARY_CARRY_FORWARD_GUARD=false`.
   The step caps that ran out are now scaled (`authorMaxStepsFor`, `findingsMergeMaxStepsFor` — the findings-merge agent's flat 20 lost a whole validation round on a 10-chapter volume after 34 read/grep calls), and the harness's truncation-guard warning goes through `logLine` so it actually reaches `summary.log` (it used to be a `console.warn`, which is why volume 04's cause was invisible in the log file and visible only in the per-turn chat dump). Related: gotcha 58 (an agent that cannot read what it must preserve starts reconstructing it from memory — this is the same failure at the other end of the size range), gotcha 14 (the 64 KB truncation of the injected reference), gotcha 43 (a silent truncation is a silent omission), gotcha 65 (the same failure in the other two cumulative stages, plus the two things that made it invisible: a guard that called an improvement a loss, and a QA loop that could not tell a rewrite from a no-op).

65. **A step-cap warning is a workflow shape, not a model weakness — and a QA loop that cannot tell a rewrite from a no-op pays for the same document twice.** The character-voice and style-guide stages never got gotcha 64's fix, and the live run showed why they needed it. Volume 01's character-voice feedback turn made **46 tool calls — 29 reads, 15 searches, ZERO writes** — spent 2.63M tokens, and ended `finish_reason=tool-calls` at a flat `maxSteps: 30` while it was still verifying findings, because the one write it had been told to do was the LAST thing in its instructions ("read the source in full", "verify every fix", then "write the complete corrected files"). `character-voice.md` and `pov-map.md` were never touched. Three consequences, and each one needed its own fix:
   - **Nothing detected it.** Both files existed and were real, so `assertWroteWithFallback` reported no gap, no recovery turn ran, `assertRealOutput` passed, and the loop recorded the turn as a normal iteration and started iteration 2 — which paid **8.4M tokens** to re-audit a document that had not moved. Every existing check asks "is the file there?"; none asked "did this pass do anything?". The fix is `fingerprintFiles` (utils/fs.js): the shared loop hashes the artifacts before `runFeedback` and again after it, and byte-identical means the pass produced nothing — log loudly, record `stalled: true`, stop. The four chunked loops run the same check inline.
   - **The prompt demanded the impossible shape.** Both stages' system and user prompts said `writeFile (complete contents, overwrite)` for a cumulative document, which contradicted what the code now instructs and is the instruction that broke the glossary (gotcha 64). The prompt files now name the files and defer HOW they are written to the turn instructions (`voiceWriteInstruction` / `styleWriteInstruction`), which is what `glossary.md`'s prompts already did. The feedback prompts also stopped saying "read the following materials **in full**" and started saying "read selectively, batch your checks (one grep can cover several findings), apply each fix as soon as it is confirmed" — and the turn preamble now orders the work HIGH → MEDIUM → LOW, so a turn that runs out of steps still left a better document behind. The step caps are scaled (`voiceAuthorMaxSteps`, `styleAuthorMaxSteps`, and `findingsMergeMaxStepsFor` in both chunked loops) instead of a flat 30 / 20. The seed + index + edit-in-place rules themselves are the same four rules gotcha 64 lists, now in all three cumulative stages.
   - **The acceptance loop was buying its own second sample at the most expensive price available.** `ACCEPTANCE_MIN_SAMPLES` is `min(2, ACCEPTANCE_WINDOW_SIZE)`, so volume 01's voice reference scored **76/100 against a passing score of 69 — a passing grade — and still could not accept**, because the window needed two samples and had one. The only route to the second sample was a feedback rewrite plus a full re-audit: 2.63M + 8.4M tokens for evidence a 55k-token grade produces. `confirmPassingScore` (`ACCEPTANCE_CONFIRM_ON_PASSING`, default ON) takes the missing samples from the same artifact — one at the judging temperature, the last at temperature 0 — and puts them INTO the window. It is not a lower copy of the exceptional path: that one keeps its confirmations out of the window on purpose, because a failed consensus must lead to the ordinary loop and not to an acceptance built from the grades that just failed. This one is filling the window, so a re-grade that FAILS is the correct outcome — it is the signal that the feedback round the loop was about to run is genuinely wanted.
   **The guard itself had a false positive, and it was destroying good work.** `compareGlossaryCarryForward` compared term cells as exact strings, so an agent that WIDENED a row with a new alias looked like one that deleted it. Volume 02 was quarantined for "dropping" two terms that were both still in the file: `三つ編み魔王 / 三つ編み悪魔 / 呪われし姫君` had become `三つ編み魔王 / 三つ編み悪魔 / 魔王 / 呪われし姫君`, and `鈍感系巻き込まれ型主人公` had gained two aliases — a glossary that had grown 88 terms → 140, thrown away. The gate now compares entries at SPELLING resolution (`glossaryTermSpans`): an entry is carried when every spelling its term column named is still present somewhere (its own row, a longer row, or split across several), and `restructured` counts those so a reword is visible instead of being counted as a loss. The same lesson shaped the other two gates: the character-voice gate keys on `voicePrimaryName` (the persona tag `【俺人格】` is the half a feedback pass is most likely to reword, and a heading COUNT dropping is what proves a real deletion), and the style-guide gate checks only the `## ` category set the prompt specifies and REPORTS a drop in the rule count rather than failing on it. The rule to keep: **a carry-forward gate protects the terminology, not the formatting of the rows that held it** — a guard that calls an improvement a loss quietly destroys the work the pipeline paid for, and it does so while looking like it is enforcing rigor.

66. **A running Node process cannot pick up a code change, so the reload boundary is the process — and the pipeline already had one.** The self-repair idea this project is moving toward needs a way for a fixed module to become the code the rest of the run executes. In-process hot-swapping does not work here, for three reasons that are all specific to this codebase: `gulpfile.js` requires all ten task modules at the top of the file, so Node's module cache holds them for the whole run; every task module captures its settings at load time (`const seriesDir = process.env.SERIES_LOCATION` in glossary.js:76, `const tiebreakBand = …` in verify-translate.js:155), which is why every test spawns a child per task; and evicting one module from the cache leaves the new module beside every old reference already captured — gulpfile's destructured `const { glossary }`, the re-export chains between `translate.js` / `verify-translate.js` / `utils/translate.js`, and any agent session mid-turn — which produces a volume half-built by old code and half by new. That is not a fix; it is a new bug class of exactly the kind this list is full of. The fix is `index.js`: **one step per process**, which is cheap here for a reason worth remembering — the idempotent skip-checks make a re-run nearly free, and a process start is NOT a model container switch (`hooks/.model-switch-state` makes a repeat switch a no-op, gotcha 22), so the expensive thing in this pipeline is a container switch, not a process. Two consequences to keep: **(a) a patch may not land mid-step.** The cumulative tasks' carry-forward gates compare a volume's artifact against the previous volume's; if the code changed between them, a "loss" may be the code change rather than a data loss, and the gate would quarantine good work (gotcha 65). Fix loops belong at step boundaries. **(b) the skip checks do not know the code changed.** They key on source fingerprints, rolling state and `contextHash` — none of which include a code version — so after a patch a plain re-run skips everything and the fix never executes. The primitive that makes a patch take effect is deleting the affected volume's outputs AND its `*-rolling-state.json` (`wipeAttemptOutputs` already does exactly that, deliberately), which is also what sets `regeneratedAny` and rebuilds the tail. `--force --volume NN` does NOT cascade (the volume filter puts one volume in the loop, so later volumes stay built on the old one) — quarantine-and-cascade is the correct primitive, not force. See §3.6.
67. **The expensive failures in this pipeline are not crashes, and a runner that reacts only to exceptions would have caught none of them.** Every failure this list describes was found by a human reading `.logs/` the next morning: 457 glossary terms vanished with no error (gotcha 64); volume 06 shipped 411 of volume 05's 769 terms and was **accepted at 78/100**; volume 04 has a `glossary.md` and nothing else — no validation report, no coverage audit, no rolling state; a 164-character sentence about an agent's plan became the series' published character voice reference and passed `assertRealOutput`, the acceptance loop, the validation report and the last-good-volume copy walk (gotcha 58); 554 spine pages became 35 fake chapters and the pipeline translated the copyright page (gotcha 52); `TRANSLATE_CHUNK_CHARS` split 55 of 133 chapters for no reason (gotcha 57); a feedback pass made 46 tool calls, wrote nothing, and the loop paid 8.4M tokens to re-audit an unchanged document (gotcha 65). **Not one threw.** So the check that would have caught most of them is not an error handler — it is an invariant question the code never asked: *did this step leave the files this step always leaves, in the shape those files always have?* That is `utils/artifacts.js` (the declaration) + `utils/postmortem.js` (the deterministic ask), run per step by `index.js` before the next step starts. Two rules to keep. **The assessment must be deterministic-first**: 17 volumes × 9 steps is 153 assessments per run, and a model call reading log transcripts to audit things that are fine turns the auditor into the most expensive stage in the pipeline (the live run measured 465M tokens). Tier 1 costs nothing; only what it flags is worth a model call. **And the check must not over-declare.** `required` is reserved for files the pipeline writes on every path it can reach, and the known-file vocabulary must be complete — otherwise every staged book, every extracted chapter and every piece of deliberately-kept evidence becomes a finding, and a checker that fires on healthy output is a checker people learn to ignore. That is gotcha 65's lesson in its general form, and it is why `test/test-postmortem.js` asserts BOTH halves: a clean series reports nothing, and ten planted defects are all detected. See §3.6.

68. **A carry-forward gate that cannot tell a rename from a deletion will destroy the edit its own prompt asked for.** The gate fixed by gotcha 65 still read only the glossary table's **first column**. Volume 15 of the live series died there. The chain, end to end: (1) the term-extraction pass sees a *truncated* copy of the previous glossary (445 rows, 200 shown), and the relevance rule keeps a row when its term cell occurs verbatim in the text being processed (`src.includes(cells[0])` in `truncateGlossary`) — the chapter prints `双ふた花ばの恋こい物もの語がたり`, the glossary holds `双ふた花ばの恋物語`, different strings, so the row was not in the excerpt; (2) the extractor re-proposed it as a new term, despite the truncation note telling it not to (a warning about content the model cannot see is not a rule); (3) the amend agent reconciled the two spellings into one row **exactly as `system-prompts/glossary.md` instructs** — "If a new term conflicts with an existing one (e.g., the same character appears under two spellings…), reconcile them to a single canonical form and note the change" — moving the old spelling into the Notes column as "also written …"; (4) the gate searched only term cells, found the old spelling nowhere, called it a dropped term, quarantined the glossary, and with `ON_VOLUME_ERROR=abort` + `ON_TASK_ERROR=abort` stopped a 12-hour, 249M-token run and left volumes 15–17 unbuilt. **The glossary had grown from 445 terms to 460.** The gate's own message blamed the wrong thing ("A pass that rewrites the whole file cannot finish it: the cumulative glossary is larger than one reply can write"), which is why an automated fixer chasing the exception found nothing — nothing was broken except a contradiction between the prompt and the guard. Four rules now stand:
   - **The gate knows the third legitimate form.** An entry is carried when its spellings survive in the term columns (unchanged, widened, split — gotcha 65) **or** it was renamed: a row this volume produced records every old spelling somewhere in the row (`glossaryRowText` — term, rendering, Notes), or the row carries the same target-language rendering and that rendering is unique on both sides (`normalizeGlossaryRendering`). `compareGlossaryCarryForward` returns `renamed` separately, `logRenamedTerms` prints each one (`old → new [section]`), and a renamed row is not counted in `added`.
   - **The rename test only trusts a row that is this volume's new work.** A row already carrying a carried-forward term is not the row the missing entry "moved into", so an incidental mention in someone else's Notes cannot hide a real deletion. And a spelling shorter than `CARRY_FORWARD_MIN_ALIAS_SPAN_CHARS` (2) proves nothing by substring matching — a one-character term occurs in half the Notes cells of a real glossary.
   - **The validator is told renames are expected output.** `system-prompts/glossary-validator.md` has a "Renames are expected output" section and a `Renamed Entries` report section: a rename is not a dropped term, the fix is never "add the old spelling back as a second row" (that manufactures the duplicate the one-canonical-rendering rule exists to prevent, and the consistency check would then flag it), and the one thing the validator *can* check — because it has this volume's source — is whether the new spelling is a form the source actually prints. `glossary-feedback.md` carries the matching rule so a feedback pass does not undo the rename.
   - **The failure message now reads the evidence.** It says whether the file shrank (the shape of a reply-budget cut-off) or did not (the shape of a replaced row), instead of asserting the first.
   The same incident has two follow-ups that are NOT fixed here and are worth knowing: `truncateGlossary`'s relevance test compares the **whole term cell** against the text, so an alias row (`A / B / C`) can never match and is systematically dropped from the window (20 of 445 rows on the live series); and the extraction prompt's "do not re-add a term as new merely because it is absent from what you can see here" is unenforceable while the list it refers to is truncated. The honest fixes are span-level relevance matching and handing the extractor the complete term list (445 terms ≈ 6 KB). `test/test-glossary-load.js` pins all four rules above, in both directions: the volume-15 rename passes, a deleted entry with no trace still fails, and an incidental mention still fails.

69. **A runner with the authority to re-run steps needs a memory of what it already tried, and a test that exercises that runner must not be able to touch the work it delivers.** Two halves, learned in the same sitting.

    **The spinning half.** The delivery-manager design hands an agent authority to re-run a step, wipe a volume's outputs and cascade, force a re-audit. Its predictable failure is not danger — it is **spinning**: a finding appears, the step is re-run, the identical finding appears again, it is re-run again, and a 12-hour run ends where it started. Volume 15 (gotcha 68) is exactly this shape: re-running a deterministic gate produces the identical quarantine every time, because the finding was never about the data. Nothing in the pipeline could see this, because everything that persists — `translation-state.json`, the `*-rolling-state.json` files, the verification sidecars, the provenance sidecars — records **artifacts**, and none of them records a **decision**. So `utils/ledger.js` is the run's memory, and the rule it enforces is `TRANSLATE_QA_RETRY_BUDGET` lifted to run level: the same action against the same finding, ending `unchanged` or `worse` twice in one run, is refused, and the refusal names the next step (open a ticket) rather than just saying no. Three properties keep it from becoming the guard that gets switched off (gotcha 65's lesson again): only *repetition* is blocked — a different action or a different volume is a new attempt; an attempt that `improved` is not evidence against anything; and the count is scoped to one run, so a real fix does not freeze an action out forever. `recurringFindings` is the free half of the same trigger: a finding class that survived an earlier recorded run is structural, and `index.js` now refuses to print the old unconditional advice "Re-run (the idempotent skip-checks make a re-run cheap)" when its own ledger says a re-run has already not cleared it. Two directions to keep: **a ledger that cannot be read is treated as spinning, not as empty** (refusing an action is recoverable — fix the file and it is available again; spinning is not), and `readLedger` never reports a corrupt or half-parsed ledger as empty (the `readUsableManifest` rule, gotcha 33).

    **The test half.** The first version of `test/test-ledger.js` spawned a real pipeline step with only `AI_API_KEY` blanked — and `SERIES_LOCATION` still came from `.env`. The child read the **live 17-volume series**, reused its committed manifest, and wrote a `translation-report.md` (plus `.json` and a provenance sidecar) at the series root. No model call happened, and the files were harmless empty reports, but a test had modified the corpus it is supposed to be protecting. The rule: **a test that exercises the runner must point at a fixture, not at whatever `.env` says** — blanking the key is not enough, because most tasks never reach the check that needs it (`translationReportStep` calls `getTranslationTarget()` directly and never calls `validateRequiredEnv`). `test/test-ledger.js` now builds a throwaway one-volume series with a committed schema-2 manifest, which is also what keeps the intake agent out of the picture: `readUsableManifest` returns the fixture's plan, so no model call is even attempted, and the suite asserts the words `running the intake agent` never appear in the child's output. Building that fixture surfaced a real gate worth knowing: `validateVolumeIntegrity` requires `integrity.basis` to be **≥ 20 characters** (a gate the intake agent can pass by saying nothing is not a gate), so a fixture that writes `basis: "fixture"` is silently rejected and the intake agent runs instead — offline, it then retries three times with 10 s sleeps and fails 20 s later with a message that points at the fixture's own manifest.
    **The same rule has a second half: a test that pins the context window must pin the output cap
    with it.** `test-fake-backend.js` and `test-translate-stage.js` set `AI_CONTEXT_WINDOW` (16000 /
    32000) and asserted the derived cap, but left `AI_MAX_TOKENS` to whatever `.env` said — and the
    live `.env` now sets it to 131,072. A suite whose pinned window is a twelfth of the cap it
    inherited asked for an answer eight times the size of its own window, so the harness compacted the
    agent session before the first turn, which inserted an extra request at `requests[0]` — and the
    suite asserts both that `requests[0]` is the agent's first turn and that `maxTokens === 4000`.
    The fix is `process.env.AI_MAX_TOKENS = ""` at the top of the suite, **not** `delete`: `dotenv`
    runs at `harness.js:43` and will not overwrite a variable that already exists, so an empty string
    blocks `.env` while `parseInt("")` → `NaN` keeps `envMaxTokens()` deriving from the window the
    suite means to test. Deleting the variable removes the pin and lets `.env` win — which is the same
    failure in a new costume.

70. **A rule about what an agent may choose has to sit on the thing that offers the choice, and a request for help must not state the result it wants.** The delivery-manager design gives an agent authority to act on a run without ever reading the code, and the moment it stops working it calls in the teams that CAN read the code. Two things about that conversation are load-bearing, and both come from volume 15 (gotcha 68).
    - **A ticket asks a question; it never states the result it wants.** "Make volume 15's glossary pass" is not a request for diagnosis, it is an instruction handed to people who know the code — and the cheapest way to satisfy it is always the one that removes the *finding* rather than the *fault*. In that incident the finding was a gate correctly refusing a glossary that had GROWN from 445 terms to 460, and the cheapest answer was `GLOSSARY_CARRY_FORWARD_GUARD=false` — the exact setting under which 457 terms once vanished with no error (gotcha 64). So `validateTicketShape` refuses the demand shapes (a sentence that commands, a clause that names the finding disappearing as the goal, a summary with no question in it) and each refusal says what to write instead. Be honest about what this is: a **shape** check, not a mind reader. It catches the forms a demand actually takes; the acceptance test (comparing the deliverable before and after) is what catches a demand phrased cleverly.
    - **The banned-option filter sits on the option generator, not on the chooser.** A manager given a menu picks the cheap item on it. That is not a flaw to be fixed by instructing the manager to choose wisely — instructing it is the same rule written in prose, where nothing enforces it. So `attachOptions` runs `optionIsBanned` on the diagnostics team's reply and refuses, in code: disabling a carry-forward guard, `--allow-fail` / `--allow-no-glossary`, lowering `PASSING_SCORE` or any acceptance threshold, deleting `.rejected` files or reports, editing `hooks/`, declaring an artifact expectation away, flipping an `ON_*` run policy to `skip`, and disabling the ledger. Every refusal names where it goes instead — **the account owner**, the only role that may un-check a guard — and the refused option is KEPT on the ticket, because a dropped option looks like it was never thought of and the complaint about a guard is information.
    - **The filter is narrower than "is this a good idea", and that is deliberate.** `Add the old spelling back as a second row` is not banned: it manufactures a duplicate, which is a judgment about the deliverable, not a guard being removed, and the thing that rejects it is the before/after comparison, not this filter. `test/test-tickets.js` pins that split rather than smoothing it over, and pins the false-positive half too — seven legitimate options must pass the filter, because a filter that refuses everything is the filter that gets switched off (gotcha 65's lesson in its general form). Related rules: a ticket's "already tried" list is read out of the ledger rather than asserted from memory, so a spin cannot be laundered into a fresh request; and a ticket closes only as `improved` / `unchanged` / `worse`, never as `finding-gone`.

71. **A triage that reads a half-built run has to tell "a gate fired" apart from "work is missing" — and a declared artifact name is a template, not a file name.** `utils/resume.js` was written against a spec and then run against the live 17 volumes, and the live run found four bugs in it in under a second, none of which a fixture built by hand would have found. What it proposed first: **wipe volume 02's glossary and cascade 02→17** — throwing away thirteen volumes of accepted work, because a `glossary.md.rejected` was lying in volume 02's folder. Volume 02's glossary is complete; the `.rejected` is evidence from the run that got stopped, and that volume was rebuilt afterwards. Four rules, each one the fix of one of those bugs:
    - **Damage and evidence are different findings, and only damage picks the resume point.** `quarantine-present` is HIGH in `utils/postmortem.js` — correctly, a gate refused something and nobody read it — but it is not a statement that the work is unfinished. So `readWorkingState` splits the HIGH findings, and a step whose required output is complete while its evidence survives gets status `quarantined` and verdict `evidence`: *read it before running anything*. The split is written fail-closed — a HIGH finding counts as damage **unless it is explicitly listed as evidence**, so a finding kind added later to `utils/postmortem.js` cannot become invisible here by omission.
    - **A quarantine belongs to the artifact it names.** `utils/artifacts.js` declares ONE pattern (`QUARANTINED_CUMULATIVE`) for all three cumulative gates, so pattern-matching alone says a `character-voice.md.rejected` is glossary's evidence — and then the "the gate removed this output" rule fires for a volume whose glossary gate never fired, which is the wrong reason to refuse a rebuild. `quarantineBelongsToStep` strips the `.rejected` suffix and asks which step declares THAT file.
    - **`{installment}` is a placeholder, and comparing it to a directory listing never matches.** `jump-in-wiki-validation-{installment}.md` was reported missing on every volume of a series that had it, and `declaredOutputsFor` put a literal `{installment}` into a **delete list**, where it deletes nothing — which is worse than an error, because the wipe looks like it happened. `resolveDeclaredName` is now applied everywhere a declared name meets the disk.
    - **When the gate removed the output, a re-run is the spin, and the plan has to say so.** Volume 15 is missing its glossary AND holds `glossary.md.rejected` in the same folder. Re-running the step rebuilds the file and runs the same deterministic gate over it, producing the identical quarantine (gotcha 68). So that shape becomes `open-ticket`, with the reason naming the identical quarantine out loud — a refusal that does not name the spin just looks like caution, and caution is what gets switched off. The same escalation applies to a FAIL audit verdict (re-auditing unchanged artifacts reproduces the FAIL) and to a finding the ledger says already survived a recorded run.
    Two silent no-ops surfaced while testing the last one, and they are the general lesson of §3.6 and gotcha 67: `recurringFindings(entries)` was called **without a run id**, and its own body requires `runs.has(run)` — so it returned `[]` forever, and the "this finding survived a run" warning could never fire; and the renderer read `r.kind` from an object whose field is `finding`, so even when it did fire the sentence would have named nothing. A check that can never report is not a check. For a resume triage the run it means is **the newest one recorded** (`entries[entries.length - 1].run`), because the triage has no run of its own — it is reading what previous runs left.
    The fixture half is worth as much: seeding a "complete" series from `STEP_ARTIFACT_SPECS` itself is the right way to build one (the fixture and the declaration cannot drift), but `discover` **declares the plan of record as one of its outputs**, so the seeder overwrote the fixture's committed manifest and the triage correctly reported that there is no usable plan (gotcha 33). And `validateDiscoveryBlock` wants `discovery.confidence` as an **object of 0–1 numbers**, not a number — a fixture whose plan does not validate is a fixture with no plan at all, same as `integrity.basis`'s 20-character floor in gotcha 69. Finally: `postMortemDir()` is where the ledger lives, so a resume test that does not set `POSTMORTEM_DIR` is reading the real series' history, and a finding recorded there changes what the test asserts.

72. **A manager that can act has to know whether something else is writing, and its own actions have to be countable — or every guard built on top of it is decoration.** Act mode turns "the manager proposes" into "the manager does", and that turned three things that were safe by accident into things that had to be built.

    **The collision.** A manager with authority to re-run a step can start a step while a run is already working on the same folders, and the damage is invisible to every check in this file: the artifact ends half-built by one process and half by the other, and "is the file there?" says yes. So `<POSTMORTEM_DIR>/run.lock` says who is running, and `index.js` plus **every** gulp task take it before doing anything — wrapped outside `withHooks`, so a refusal costs no model container switch (gotcha 22). Nesting is counted, because the default run holds one lock across its nine in-process steps while `index.js` spawns gulp children that inherit `INDEX_RUN_ID` and join it; only the writer deletes it. Two directions, and getting them the wrong way round is the bug: the lock is **advisory to the pipeline** — a lock file that cannot be written warns and continues, because a bookkeeping file must not be the reason a 12-hour run dies — and **binding to the manager**, which treats "I cannot tell" as "no", because deciding whether it is safe to act is its whole job. Liveness is the pid, and a pid this machine cannot check (a lock written on another host) is treated as *in progress* with the file to delete named out loud: guessing "probably stale" is how a half-written volume gets a second writer. The lock lives in `POSTMORTEM_DIR`, not per series, so two concurrent runs of two different series collide — the answer is a separate `POSTMORTEM_DIR` per series, and the refusal says so in the sentence it prints.

    **The count.** Act mode records what it did, and it must record it under the **newest recorded run**, not a fresh id per invocation. A manager that invents its own run id each time is uncatchable: the anti-spin gate counts per run (gotcha 69), so every attempt would look like the first. A genuinely new `npm run pipeline` run makes its own id, and that is where the count resets. Two halves of the same rule fell out of the tests: `appendLedgerEntry` used to write `String(null)` for a null volume, storing the four-character string `"null"`, and `matchesKey` compares a step-level entry against a step-level key — so a whole-step action (`re-audit` on `consistency-audit`, which names no volume) **never matched itself** and the gate silently stopped counting the exact case it exists for; and a `re-run-step` on a step that is only in the plan because the resume step's fix invalidates it had no finding to key on, so the ledger refused it as "half-specified" — seven spurious tickets on a fixture with nothing wrong with them. A complete step in a resume plan now **inherits the finding the whole plan is a response to**, which is honest: the whole sequence is one response to one finding.

    **The sequence.** A resume plan is not a list of independent suggestions. If the step where the run stopped is escalated to a ticket, the seven steps listed after it are conditional on that ticket being answered, and running them spends a real run's worth of model calls on a foundation this manager could not fix — so act mode executes nothing in that shape, and stops at the first refusal or failed step. Related refusals, all for the same reason: no `--dry-run` passthrough in act mode and no `--no-write` with `--mode=act` (exit 2), because a rehearsal that records its interventions poisons the ledger that is supposed to catch the spin; and a Tier C move is refused **at execution time**, not only in the proposal, because the proposal is a document and execution is the thing with consequences. `test/test-delivery-act.js` pins all of it against real fixtures and real step-runner children, including the half that matters most: a refusal leaves the folder listing byte-for-byte as it was, because a refusal that wiped first is indistinguishable from a fix.

73. **"Did it help?" has to be answered by the deliverable, not by the finding — and a measurement that reads a gate's verdict can be satisfied by switching the gate off.** Act mode's first verdict rule was a per-volume count: how many of the step's volumes had their required files before, and how many after. It was replaced by `utils/delivery-verify.js`, and the reason is a case the count could not see at all. The test that exercised the plumbing wiped volume 02's glossary and ran the step with `--dry-run`, which rebuilds nothing. The count reported `unchanged` — volume 02 was missing files before and missing files after, so nothing had crossed the line — while the deliverable had actually **lost a glossary that was there**. A manager that reports a clean run after deleting accepted work is worse than a manager that refuses. Four rules, and each one is a distinct failure mode:

    - **A regression vetoes an improvement.** The planted test "fix" finishes the step (every declared file written, the next triage reports nothing to do) and writes a glossary with the term rows missing. It is recorded as `worse`, with the damage named. This is the case `utils/tickets.js` deliberately does **not** ban — `Add the old spelling back as a second row` is a judgment about the deliverable, not a guard being switched off (gotcha 70) — so the thing that has to reject it is this comparison. A rule that netted the two out would call it a win: the finding gone plus a step completed against one smaller glossary is a comfortable majority in favour.
    - **A gate's verdict is not a deliverable signal.** `consistency-report.md`'s PASS/FAIL, the four validation reports, the quarantine counts and the post-mortem's own findings are all excluded from the measurement. Every one of them moves for free when the check is disabled, which is exactly the cheapest answer a ticket gets (gotcha 70 again). Only facts about the **content** the pipeline produced are measured — how many term rows the glossary carries, how many `### Character` sections the voice reference holds, which `## ` style categories survive, how many chapters `chapters.json` hands the translator, and the publish report's own buckets. That is the difference between "the complaint stopped" and "the book got better", and it is why a weakened guard cannot be accepted as a fix even when it is honestly reported as one.
    - **An incomplete measurement is reported, never guessed.** A file that could not be read (a directory where the glossary was, an artifact over the read cap) makes that step's content signals *not comparable* — not zero. `fingerprintFiles`' rule in `utils/fs.js` is the same rule ("an unreadable file fingerprints as `missing` so a read error cannot masquerade as 'nothing changed'"), and the direction matters: treating a read failure as an empty document reports a permissions problem as a loss of terminology, which is the direction of error that gets accepted work deleted. Two related honesty rules: a corrupt `glossary-disputes.json` is not "the disputes were settled", and the median verification score is compared only when both sides graded the **same number of chapters** — a book that gained a chapter is a different book, and averaging across it is not a measurement.
    - **The protected quantity is the one the pipeline's own gate protects.** Glossary terms, `### Character` sections, `## ` style categories: the same three halves `GLOSSARY_CARRY_FORWARD_GUARD` / `VOICE_CARRY_FORWARD_GUARD` / `STYLE_CARRY_FORWARD_GUARD` guard (gotcha 64/65/68). And where those gates deliberately stop, so does this: the style guide's **rule count** is `veto: false`, because "a guide that says the same thing in fewer words is not damaged" is already the codebase's answer, and a measurement that guarded a different quantity than the gate does would report a different series of failures than the one the pipeline can actually see. A reworded character heading moves nothing here either — deciding whether a rename is a loss is the carry-forward gate's job (gotcha 68), not a heading count's.

    Two supporting changes came out of building it. The publish report is now read through **one** reader — `readTranslationReport` + `summarizeReportRows` in `utils/translation-report.js`, the module that writes the file — so the resume triage and the before/after comparison cannot count one report two ways; and the ledger stores the `signals` account next to the verdict, because a verdict with no numbers beside it is a verdict nobody can argue with afterwards, least of all the diagnostics team who is the reader this whole layer exists to serve. A ticket closes through the same comparison (`closureFromComparison`, reached from `delivery.js`'s `closeTicketOnDeliverable`), so the manager cannot record `worse` in the ledger and close the ticket `improved`. One measurement bug worth remembering: `countHeadings` was a prefix test, and `"### Character".startsWith("##")` is true — so every level-3 heading counted as a level-2 one, and then **none** of them counted when level 3 was asked for. A heading is its level marker followed by a space.

74. **A read-only agent needs its guarantee twice, and its answer needs the same filter as its question — plus a rule about what a support team is allowed to have actually read.** The diagnostics role is the first agent in this pipeline whose *whole job* is to look at the code without changing it, and three things turned out not to be automatic.

    **The two layers, and which one actually fires.** `createGatedFsTools` builds five tools and an approve gate. Handing that whole set to a read-only agent and letting the gate say no is one layer; handing it only the three read tools is another. Both are needed, and the reason the *record* is the hard part is that when the tool set is the one that refuses, the gate is never consulted at all — the provider answers `Model tried to call unavailable tool 'writeFile'` before the call reaches the sandbox, so the gate's own refusal log is empty and a naive `writeAttempts: gate.refusals` reports **"the team did not try"** for the common case where it tried and the tool set said no. `collectWriteAttempts` merges both sources (the gate's log, plus the turn's recorded tool calls whose name is a mutating one) and labels which layer stopped each. The same two-layer shape is what gotcha 8 already pins for `deleteFile`; this is that rule applied to a role whose entire contract is "look, don't touch". And the refusal must reach the model, not just the log: a denied call surfaces as a tool error and the loop continues, which is what lets the turn finish with an answer instead of dying on a refusal.

    **`AGENT_TOOLS_NOTE` is a promise about writing.** The shared note appended to every agent-mode system prompt names five tools and tells the agent to write its output with them. For a read-only role that is not a slightly-off hint, it is an instruction the role cannot obey, and a model that follows it spends capped steps reasoning about a write it is not allowed to do (gotcha 8's `deleteFile` transcripts are exactly that shape). So the diagnostics prompt carries its own `DIAGNOSIS_TOOLS_NOTE`, appended in code like every other mode-specific note (AGENTS.md convention: prompt files stay mode-agnostic), and it says what the role has, what it must not do, and the exact JSON shape to answer in.

    **A diagnosis is a claim about reading, so check the reading.** `crossCheckReads` compares the reply's `read` list against the turn's real tool calls, and the two rules that keep it from being a false-positive machine are the ones easy to get wrong: a `grep`/`listFiles` call names a **folder** and reads everything inside it (gotcha 60), so a claim about a file under that folder is *not* "cited but never opened"; and a call that **errored** covers nothing, so counting it would let a reply cite a file it only managed to fail to open. Both directions were wrong in the first version, and a check that accuses an honest diagnosis is a check the next incident routes around (gotcha 65's lesson again). The same honesty applies to the outcome-only rule: an option whose only stated check is "the finding disappears" is **flagged**, not refused, because the thing that rejects it is the before/after comparison of the deliverable (gotcha 73) — and the flag has to be stamped by `filterOptions`, on the way onto the ticket, or it exists only in the in-memory reply and never reaches `tickets.md` or the manager who has to read it.

    **The filter is the door, so make it the only door.** `recordDiagnosis` calls `attachOptions` internally. That is not tidiness: if the manager-side code could write a diagnosis without going through the filter, the filter would be a suggestion (gotcha 70). Two consequences worth remembering: when every offered option was refused, the ticket keeps status `answered` and gains `noUsableOptions` rather than inventing a new status — and `recordDiagnosis` writes `noUsableOptions: false` explicitly when usable options survive, because "absent" and "false" must not mean the same thing on a ticket a human reads. And the manager's side of the conversation is validated on the **answer**, not the question: the team may ask anything it needs to, but a reply may cite only what a customer is allowed to see, so `.logs/**`, `*.js`, `system-prompts/`, `hooks/` and `utils/` are refused at the point where the boundary would actually leak — with the refusal naming what the manager *may* look at, because the useful failure is the one that produces a usable second answer.

75. **A role that may change the code needs its boundary enforced twice, its claims checked against the disk, and its unfinished work reported as unfinished — and the record is where all three actually get decided.** The dev team is the last role in the delivery layer and the only one that writes, and building it produced four lessons that were each a bug first.

    **Both layers fire, and only one of them leaves a log.** `patchFsTools` hands the agent five tools and an approve gate that refuses `deleteFile` and every banned path. But a tool the harness never advertises never reaches the gate: the provider answers `Model tried to call unavailable tool 'deleteFile'` and the gate's refusal list stays empty. So the record has to be assembled from both sources, exactly like `collectWriteAttempts` for the read-only role (gotcha 74) — and the first version of `collectPatchWriteAttempts` over-corrected: it counted *every* mutating tool call the gate had not refused as a tool-set refusal, which reported the patch's own **successful** `editFile` as "not offered to this role at all". The fix is the third argument, `advertised`: a call counts as a tool-set refusal only when its name is NOT in the list the role was actually handed. The general rule: **a write that succeeded belongs in `actualChanges`** — the tree fingerprint — **not in the refusals**, and a report that mixes them makes a working patch look like an attempted break-in. The default `[]` keeps the read-only role's assumption intact, which is what `utils/diagnostics.js` relies on.

    **The harness gives an agent no shell, so "run the tests" is the machine's job.** A brief that told the team to run `npm test` would spend capped steps discovering it cannot, and a proposal that claimed it had run them would be unverifiable — the same shape as gotcha 8's `deleteFile` transcripts, where agents burned budget reasoning about a tool that does not exist. So `DEVTEAM_TOOLS_NOTE` says plainly that there is no shell and that the machine runs the checks, and `REQUIRED_CHECKS` + `runChecks` run `npm test` then `npm run pipeline-loop` from the CLI, through a shell because `npm` is a `.cmd` shim on Windows, with a 30-minute ceiling and the tail of the output stored. Two honesty rules came out of that: `normalizeChecks` takes the command from the table and never from the caller (an unpinned id is dropped, so the verdict reports it MISSING instead of trusting whatever was handed in), and **a check that never ran is reported as missing, not as failed** — "did not run" and "ran and returned 1" are different facts and the report prints them on separate lines. `judgeChecks` keeps them apart, and a failing re-verify moves a `verified` patch back to `proposed` rather than leaving a stale green badge on it.

    **The banned-path table is necessary and not sufficient, and its refusals have to name the rule.** A patch may not edit the guard tables, the tests that pin them, `hooks/`, `.env`, the machine state or the corpus — enforced on the file tools during the turn AND on the proposal afterwards, because a team that gets past one layer has not been stopped. Three details are load-bearing. (a) **Specific rule first, catch-all last:** `hooks/pre-glossary.sh` must be refused as `edit-hooks`, not as "corpus", because the refusal is the information the team uses to write a second proposal. (b) **A path that cannot be normalised into the project is banned, not allowed** — and `normalizeProjectPath` resolves an ABSOLUTE path against the root it was given, because `renderTicketForDev` names the project root in full and an agent that used the path it was handed was being refused for every legitimate write. A path genuinely outside the root still returns null. (c) **The project-source test is a whitelist, not a blacklist:** `ai-client/` holds the run's generated output as well as its code, so "allowed unless listed" defaults to editing the evidence the acceptance test measures. What the table cannot see at all is a patch that leaves `package.json` untouched and quietly drops a suite from `scripts.test` — `package.json` is not a banned file — so `readTestChain` before and after the turn plus `testChainIsIntact` is the second check: removing a suite is a refusal, adding one is a warning.

    **An unjudged patch is live code, and that changes what the manager may do next.** `fix.js` works in `main`, not a branch (the branch idea was dropped 2026-10-05: one team at a time, and a patch may not land while a run is in progress — gotcha 66's rule that a carry-forward gate cannot tell a code change from a data loss). The patch stays uncommitted while the manager judges it, because the commit IS the acceptance act and only the dev team makes it. Which means a `proposed` patch is already in the tree, so act mode refuses the whole plan while `unresolvedPatches()` is non-empty: running a step now would produce a before/after measurement that cannot say whether the patch or the step moved it. Two consequences of that, both found while testing. The "the tree already holds changes no patch declares" refusal had to move **inside `workTicket`, after the tree is read and before `createPatch`** — a refusal that opened the patch first left a dangling `proposed` record gating act mode for a change nobody proposed. And `--verify` only **warns** about undeclared changes, because it mutates nothing; refusing there would make the read-only command stricter than the mutating one. Related: a refused proposal attempt is **history, not current state** (`refusedAttempts`, with `problems` cleared when a later attempt lands — otherwise the report describes a patch that no longer has that problem); judging a patch is not a ledger entry (the patch record is the record, and the ledger records the consequence, which is the wipe-and-cascade); and a judgment reason is checked, not just required — "I accepted it because volume 15 passes now" is refused, because that is the complaint stopping, not the change being sound (gotcha 70 again).

    **Git has to be asked, not guessed, and this repository is the reason.** The git root is the parent `oresuki/` folder, so git paths appear as `ai-client/…`, and the working tree outside `ai-client/` holds ~800 deliberate changes that belong to the account owner's in-progress translation output. So every git call is path-scoped (`git status --porcelain -uall -- <relative root>`, `git add -- <the patch's declared files>`, `git commit -m … -- <files>`) and `git add -A` is never used: it would commit somebody else's work. `-uall` is required because plain `--porcelain` collapses a brand-new folder into a single entry, and both the "extra changes" list and `revertPatch`'s `leftBehind` must name FILES — a folder name in a list a human is supposed to act on is not actionable. `gitRootOf` asks `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` instead of guessing from the folder layout, because a patch record written in a subfolder of a repository whose root is elsewhere is exactly the case where guessing produces a "clean tree" reading of a dirty one.

    **The fixture half, because a dev-team test is a git test.** A fixture repo needs its own `git config user.email` / `user.name` / `commit.gpgsign false` (the identity has to be inherited by `commitPatch`'s own git calls), its patch records kept in a channel folder OUTSIDE the repo (otherwise the records are undeclared changes in the tree being fingerprinted), and its own `package.json` carrying BOTH pinned scripts — `npm` resolves the nearest `package.json` walking UP the tree, so a fixture without one runs the real project's test chain from a temp folder. And the fixture's `glossary.js` is the volume-15 bug on purpose (gotcha 68's whole-cell relevance match), because the first real job for this team is the two unfixed halves of that incident: `truncateGlossary` comparing the whole term cell instead of the aliases inside it, and the extraction prompt's unenforceable "do not re-add a term as new merely because it is absent from what you can see here".


76. **A loop that drives the pipeline has to call the commands, not the functions — and a menu entry with no command behind it is a report, not a menu.** `autopilot.js` is the last piece of the delivery layer, and four things it needed turned out to be missing pieces of the pieces.

    **A code fix takes effect at a process boundary, so the loop spawns commands.** The first shape of the loop was `while (action.type !== "end") { switch (action.type) { run | diagnose | fix } }` calling `delivery.runActPlan(...)` in-process. That is wrong for this codebase specifically: Node caches a module the first time it is required, `gulpfile.js` requires all ten task modules at the top of the file, and `delivery.js` is not a banned patch path — so a patch that changed `utils/prompt.js` or the gates themselves would have been invisible to a loop that had already required them, and the loop would have gone on executing the **pre-patch** gates for the rest of the run. That is gotcha 66's failure class wearing a new hat. Spawning the account owner's own command per action is the reload boundary, it is cheap here because the idempotent skip-checks make a re-run nearly free, and it has a second benefit: the loop cannot reach past a gate, because there is no gate to reach past — it can only type the command. Related, and settled the same way: the loop takes **no run lock of its own**. `delivery.js --mode=act` takes one under the newest recorded run, so a loop-wide lock would make its own children refuse; and holding a lock across the whole loop would claim "a run is in progress" during the `fix` branch, which is exactly the case gotcha 66 forbids (a patch may not land while a run is working on the volumes). What the loop does instead is check `runInProgress()` before each action and stop if something else started.

    **Three menu entries had no command behind them.** `DELIVERY_ACTIONS` listed `open-ticket`, and `planResume` proposed it, and nothing executed it: `executableSteps` returns `[]` for a plan whose resume point is a ticket, so act mode ran nothing and only a *gate refusal* ever reached `openTicketFor`. `recordChoice` was reachable only from code, so an answered ticket could be read but not acted on — which meant the dev team, summoned only by an option marked `requiresCodeChange`, could never be summoned at all. And there was no entry for choosing, judging a patch, or sending the dev team. The fixes are `delivery.js --open-ticket` and `delivery.js --choose=<optionId> --ticket=<id> --reason=<text>`, three new Tier A entries (`choose-option`, `dev-team-patch`, `judge-patch`, all `countsAsIntervention: false` because the counted consequence is the wipe-and-cascade that applies a patch), and `ACTION_MENU_ENTRIES` in `utils/manager.js` mapping every decision the manager may name to the entry that gives it a command — pinned by a test, because "the two menus are different lists on purpose" is exactly the situation where drift is invisible. Two details that are not details: `--open-ticket` writes the question from `questionForEscalation`, keyed on `plan.escalation`, rather than `openTicketFor`'s "has been tried and the deliverable did not move" — the triage **refused** to try, so the old wording would have stated an attempt that never happened, and a ticket whose "already tried" list is a fiction gets answered by switching the guard off (gotcha 70). And it is **idempotent**: a live ticket for that exact step/volume/finding is named rather than duplicated, so the loop may call it every iteration; two tickets for one complaint put the same question in front of the diagnostics team twice and make the ledger's account describe neither.

    **A triage that cannot read its own correspondence re-escalates the same shape forever.** `utils/resume.js` read the ledger but never the tickets or the patches, so the plan's `open-ticket` verdict was purely disk-shaped: after the dev team landed a fix, the next triage saw the same disk shape and escalated the same question again. So `readWorkingState` now attaches `tickets` / `patches` (kept on the state so `planResume` stays a **pure function of a snapshot** — a hand-built test state must not silently read the live series' history, which is gotcha 71's trap in a new form), and an accepted or committed patch answering a ticket **supersedes** the escalation: the plan becomes `run` / `wipe-and-cascade` with a reason naming both the ticket and the patch. The supersede is deliberately asymmetric, and the asymmetry is the lesson: it applies to the three "re-running reproduces the same result" checks and **not** to `intervention-budget`, because a code change cannot un-spend attempts this run already made, and applying the patch is itself the counted wipe-and-cascade. A test caught that one — routing the budget escalation through the same `escalate()` helper made it supersede-able, which would have refunded attempts the run had already spent.

    **The manager's decision has to be a call with no tools, and its answer has to be a shaped record.** Everything else in this layer is deterministic; the only thing that needs a model is "of the moves available to me, which one now?". Handing it an agent handle would hand it file-reading tools, and the whole "never sees the code" guarantee (AGENTS.md §3.6) would become a sentence in a prompt instead of a capability it does not have — the same two-layer lesson as gotcha 74 and gotcha 75, in the direction where the role is not allowed to read at all. So `managerDecision` is `harness.runOneShot` with no `tools`, one message in, one fenced JSON block out, `parseManagerAction` fail-closed, and `validateManagerAction` refusing anything the records do not support. Two consequences worth naming. `end` is proved, not believed: `endIsProvable` checks the triage's verdict, open tickets, unjudged patches, and the deliverable's `missing` / `unverified` counts, because "I think we are done" is the claim a loop would otherwise exit on. And an unattended **accept** is gated by `safeToAcceptAutomatically` rather than by the manager's judgment, because in an unattended loop *every* accept is unattended — ordinary project code, pinned checks green, no warning, nothing escalated in prose, no measured regression, or the loop stops and names the account owner. Rejecting needs no gate: it is the direction that undoes work. Both are followed by the command that finishes them, because accepting is not landing a change (`fix.js --commit`) and rejecting is not undoing one (`fix.js --revert`) — and a rejected-but-not-reverted patch sits in `unresolvedPatches()` and gates the next run.

    **Watch mode is the only safe rehearsal, and a preview has to say it is a preview.** Act mode refuses `--no-write` and refuses `--dry-run` passthrough because a recorded intervention that did nothing poisons the ledger that exists to catch a spin (gotcha 72).
